main path up to the Miimmelsee, and he crawled under its lee. He had, lain in his dry hiding-place for perhaps five minutes, listening to the swish of the rain as it beat upon the ground, when an iron-shod foot grated on a rock. Turning his head, Lincoln saw an ill-looking fellow, in the characteristic dress ' a the Black Forest peasant, advancing with =Alen along the lateral track. His rough coat was turned up at the collar as a defence from the beating rain ; his eyes scouted to right and left ; he walked like a man limiting a trail. Lincoln lay close, and was not deteeted. The fellow passed out of sight. A. minute later, there came to the watcher's ears the melancholy hoot of an owl ; and instantly be was seized with a curious sense of foreboding. He was uneasily impressed by the stealthy movements of the peasant who had passed him, and was in the act to rise and ohlow noiselessly, when again he heard the sound of a. footstep. From his lair, he saw an- other man go by—a peasant too, with a coarse, brutal face, and a thickset frame. This one walked boldly. with no' attempt at concealment and it flashed into the watcher's mind that the first had been a scout to see that the way was clear. Lincoln waited a minute, then ruse, and followed cautiously. The rain was ceasing ; shafts of sunshine were again piercing the clouds ; a refreshing, aromatic scent of pine tilled the air. The young artist put aside the thought of the Milinmelsee. determi to follow to its end this new adventure. The narrow path curved among high rocks, and Lin • coin feared, if be advanced too fast, to treed upon the heels of his quarry. Presently the ended, and the path ran .straight through thicket of young pines, closely planted. The vista made by the cutting in the trees was blocked by something so strange that Lineoln paused, and drew a quick breath of astonishment. The men he was tracking were nowhere to he seen ; but at the moment this made little im- pression upon his mind, so occupied was he with the amazing thing that filled the daylight iipac at the end of the green tunnel through the trees. For an instant he recalled Sinbad the Sailor, -and the gigantic roc : for the thing that be saw re- sembled a huge bird poised to begin a flight. Skeleton wings, clothed with diaphonoua fabric, were outstretched motionless ; where the hire's body should be, was a light cage, with what looked like the figure of a wan; and to the watcher's ears there seemed to come the regular beating of an engine. Astonished greatly, Lin- coln hurried on down the cutting in the wood. The path was perhaps a quarter of a mile long, and he had gone but half the distance when the vast wings stirred, and beat the air with quick, powerful strokes. The bird rose a little way into the air ; there was a monstrous snorting :Ind whirring, and the huge thing was triivehlintz rapidly away from him. Lincoln darted forward at a run. At the abrupt edge of the clink wood, he pulled up, seized with a sudden bewilderment. A curi- ous scene spread before his eyes. He found him- self at the upper edge of a narrow V-shaped val- ley, scooped out among the mountains by a glacier-chisel of the dim past. The bottom was fiat, and bore a growth of rich grass ; the sides ascended at a steep angle up to the pine-clad heights. Half wax down, perhaps a mile from where lie stood, was a substantial timber house. These things Lincoln noted in a flash, scarce knowing that he saw them ; for all the energies of his mind were concentrated on the strange phenomenon that was happening in the valley- bed.

When life began again for Clive Lincoln, he met a pair of friendly blue eyes smiling into They seemed oddly familiar ; so did the tunic faced with eilver.

" Ach ! My friend. You did not get as far as the Miimmelsee after all ?" It was the officer who bad stopped him on the road. Lincoln smiled back again. " What's up ?" be asked. " I feel strangely weak. Why ! in bed !" _

It was Selma who came out from the shadows of the curtains, and talked to him sweetly irl her cooing cot tralto, that seemed to bring with it strength and peace. "You have been very brave and good, Mr. Lincoln. and we thank von with all our hearts—

Close to where he gtood was a platform of wood, whence extended a . double lino. of . rails,

which ran the whole length of the vale' dimin- ishing to a perspective point in the distance. Alongside were tall posts at regular intervals, with horizontal and transverse wires, the whole much resembling the 'arrangements for electric tramways to be seen in large cities. Racing :long this avenue of wire was the great bird, which Lincoln had seen from the other side of the wood. Its extended Wings beat the air with strong, plunging strokes' and marcellons to see,. the body of he bird was lifted in the air, several feet above the tramway, so that the upper wires alone appeared to prevent the ponderous creature from soaring into the skies as high as the moun- tain-tops. As Lincoln watched this extraordinary sight, the bird reached a point midway down the val- ley, and just opposite the house. Suddenly some- thing seemed to go wrong. One of the wings failed, drooped, stopped ; the bird turned side- ways, and with a clatter of metal collapsed help- less on the rails. Lincoln bounded forward again with the vague idea of rendering assistance to the speck of a human being, who, from his cage, had seemed to direct this sky-threatening portent. He ran fast, and was close upon the wreck of the machine, when he became aware that a girl had darted down from the balcony of the house, and was racing him to the scene of the disaster. With a sidelong glance he could study her as she ran obliquely to his own path. Tall, slight, clothed all in amber-yellow, she moved with the elastic freedom of a doe—her clear-cut face pale, some tendrils of dark hair escaping, and floating on the wind. Their speed was timed so that they met at the ruin of the collapsed bird. Entangled in a mesh of metal, where a vicious little engine still whitred helplessly, lay an elder- ly man, with fine, distinguished features, and the air of a scholar. He smiled up at the girl as she bent over him ; but his delicately-formed lips twitched with a mortification he could not wholly conceal. " Father ! Father !" came the appeal, as she put her hand in his : " you are not hurt ?" " Little, if at all, my darling," was the reply. " Only the engine is pressing down my leg." " Let me help you, said Lincoln, stepping forward. The girl appeared to see him for the arst time, so full had been her mind of her father's danger. The old man looked up at him with a delightful and undiminished dignity. Lincoln tack a good grasp of the engine; putting out his strength, he lifted it so that the prisoner could get free, then silently proffered further aid. The girl, her large eyes still wide and startled, supported her father on the other side. " Sir," said the old gentleman, turning to Lincoln, " you have come at the moment of my , defeat, but mark me, I shall conquer yet !" A vision rose before his eyes ; the hand that rested on Lincoln's arm twitched and thrilled. " Of course, father !" cried the girl; "every- thing was going splendialy. a large coniregation. Mr. John Taylor, L.D.S , pre- aided, and expressed the great pleasure it afforded him so be present, and esnecially to welcome Mr. Harbottle, an old friend %s 110 WAS a minister of the circuit at the time when he (Mr. Taylor) was a circuit steward. The LECTURER, who had a hearty reception, said it was sometimes desirable in giving a lecture not to have known the man, inasmuch as if he did not present a correct portrait no one would be aware of the fact. In the case of a contemporary like Peter Mackenzie, who was so well known, it was absolutely necessary to pre- sent a recognizable portrait. No man was more a "child of nature ' than Mackenzie, and certainly if he could express a wish he would like to be pictured just as he was, with all his peculiar characteristics. Mr. Har- bottle then dealt with Mackenzie's early life, his hard up-bringing, his work for years as a farmer's boy, during which period he learned things that helped him in after-life both in practical benevolence and in the striking figures of speech with which he was accustomed to delight his audiences. Having alluded to Mackenzie's life as a miner, which commenced when he was twenty years of age, and to his conversion which took place under the guidance of a distinguished Wesleyan layman, the lecturer pictured some of Mackenzie's work as a Sunday school teacher, a local preacher, and a missionary to the miners of the North, following him in the three stages through which, as a rule, men passed into the Wesleyan ministry. Incident- ally the lecturer mentioned that they owed Mackenzie's preservation for Methodism to the Church of England. Soon after his conversion Mackenzie was desirous of going abroad and some friends went to the Vicar of the neighbourhood in order to get his emigration papers signed. When the Vicar found they were Methodists he would have nothing to do with the matter, so that the Churchlof England which gave them John Wesley— and they would never be able to repay her for that— gave them Peter Mackenzie, the miner's apostle, also. (Applause.) The lecturer then gave personal reminis- cences of his own association with Mackenzie and his knowledge of him. In the North of England, Mackenzie was chiefly instrumental in bringing about a great revival of religion, particularly in the Gateshead cir- cuit, and at that time he gave him (Mr. Harbottle) his ticket of trial of membership. It was a strange audience which he was accustomed to gather. All classes of people, men who never darkened the door of a place of worship, were there— drawn by the wonderful preaching of Mackenzie, and I where he failed to arrest them and bring them to a re- ligious life, his brilliant 'colleague who became his biographer—Mr. Dawson—succeeded, and won many for Christ. THE WAY TO A MYSTERY,

C. N. Ama A. M. WILLIAMSON,

(AlitAcre of " The Woman in Grey," 4-e.)

The fuel( r Clive Lincoln walked, the strange; did it seem to him that he met no one on Oaf road. The swelling green valley between tli4 mountains was populous with a picturesquely. dressed peasantry ; and in the three or four timber-built villages through which he had passed since leaving Ottenhofen, he had seea women working in the fields, men striking- iron in the forges, or sawing fragrant pine trunks in the saw mills. But as 80011 as he went beyond the limits of the villages, he left human life be- hind him. He moved in At great empty land-

scape. This was the first time that Clive Lincoln had trod a German road, and his spirit thrilled with the sense of the new and the unexpected. At twenty- five the o ()rid is wonderfully fresh and surpris- ing; and the young man carried all his fottune in his eyes and on ilifi back, where was bung a folding easel, an artist's stool and a. box of col- ours. Already he had made a striking success with a " one-man exhibition" in Bond Street, of water colours of Devonshire scenery ; on the money that this had brought hint lie was under- taking his first journey to the continent. bent,,on painting the Black Forest. The Black Forest ! There was witchery in the very words, the glamour of poetry, of legend, of romance. One of Lincoln's earliest recollections was a picture of the liftimmelsee, which his mother used to show him in an early Victorian volume of steel engravings. A group of maidens sporting in the waters of a dine, pin-fringed lake were rushing to shelter as a great giant with Jove-like hair and enormous spreading arms, rose dripping from the depths. The boy had never tired of looking at the picture, and hearing from his mother the legend of the spirit of the tliim- melsee, and he had determined that the first pic- ture painted by him in Germany should be of the magic scene which had thrilled his childish im- agination. He had travelled direct from Lor- d= to the little village of Ottenhofen, and on this, the first morning after his arrival, was has- tening up into the high fastnesses of the mount- :sins to find the fairy lake. From a great distance came a strange, dull roar, like thunder —such thunder as he had never heard in England ; thunder that was curiously rythmical, seeming to issue simultaneously from opposite quarters of the compass. Lincoln lis- tened wonderingly, turning his head this way and that, looking up into the clouds ; and at last he set down the odd effect to some freakish echo, which, in mountain lands plays fantastic tricks, tossing a sound from side to side until it wearies, and lets it drop in silence. He swung on at a quick walk, his iron-shod stick of oak ring- ing musically on the road in unison with his steps. The winding way led ever upward. He was leaving the green-clad slopes and was mounting to a laud of barren rock. slopes, he met no human being—not a labourer at work; no creaking ox-team ; not a tourist. He had the great, green, blowing world to himself, and walk- ing all alone in these wide, unfamiliar solitudes, even though it was broad day, a little chill crept Into his heart. _

Suddenly he glanced up in surprise; for on a knoll above the road, he spied the figure of a tall man, clad all in blue, who stood erect, motion- less, like a sentinel. It was too far to discern the details of his dress, but Lincoln caught a gleam of silver streaking the blue, and a hint of tossing feathers crowning a burnished head-dress. The face was towards him, but the head thrown back, carried the man's glance upwards over Lincoln to the mountain tops, and where the eyes should have been, there goggled two great, black discs. As he advanced, the figure on the hill-side began to resolve itself into its component parts. The blue became tunic and trousers ; the gleam of silver, facings on the coat ; the tossing feathers, a plume upon a helmet ; the goggling discs, tbe lenses of a pair of field glasses held to the eyes by black-gloved bands : Lincoln was gazing at a Prussian officer.

The grating of his nailed boots on the road was carried by a sudden flow of wind to the soldier's ears ; he abruptly lowered his glasses, and dropping his chin, stared frowningly down. The young Englishman looked calmly back at him. A flash of resentment lit the frank Teutonic eyes. With an angry wave of the gloved hand. the officer signed to Lincoln to halt. The imperi- ous gesture was unmistakeable; but the Eng- lishman ignored it and kept on at his steady four miles an hour. _ _

A red spot glowed suddenly on each cheek of the Prussian. Holding himself very stiffly, he shouted a peremptory command in staccato Ger- man. The intention was clear, but the precise purport of the words was lost on Lincoln, whose knowledge of the tongue of the Fatherland was slight. With a wave of the hand, he kept to his stride. Why should an Englishman stay upon his road because a Prussian officer chose to shout rudely at him from a hill-aide ? The officer's face reddened allover. For an in- stant he seemed undetermined; next moment he was bounding down the slope, his scabbard clanking metallically on the rocks. Taking a diagonal path, he leaped upon the road, and placed himself directly in Lincoln's way. In a gut.ural torrent, he poured out angry words. The Englishman listened intently, but could separate only two sounds—" manoeuvres " and " artillery." A light struck into his mind. He knew that the manoeuvres of the German army are carried out on a great scale, and as far as possible under conditions of actual war. He re- membered now that at intervals, on the road he had travelled, on the sides of houses awl barns, lie had seen official-looking papers posted up ; he recalled the sound of the rhythmical thunder over which he had puzzled. Things were clearer now, and he no longer wondered at the empty road. When the angry officer stopped his excited speech, Lincoln looked him in the eyes, and said simply, in his best German : "I am an Eng- lishman : I regret that I cannot quite under• stand you." The Prussian stared his wonder, and modified ids truculence. "I am a painter," went on Lin. oln ; " I wish to go to the Miinamelsee." The other broke again into voluble talk. He rinted up to the mountain tops, where the sound o the rhythmical thunder still reverberated ; he waved a band down the road ; another upwards , then described a circle in the air with both ; arit ended triumphantly with the word " verboten." Lincoln divined that the soldier was indicating the limits of the manoeuvring area, within which no one was allowed; but he did not choose to under- stand. Stolidly he said again : " I wish to go to the Miimmelsee." The Prussian grew. warm. He looked bewil- dered, then let loose once more a flood of words. Lincoln waited politely until he had finished; then, " I must go to the Miiminelsee," he said, politely. The officer bit a desperate lip, and looked quickly up and down the road. " Then go, and may the devil take you I" was the broad inter- pretation that Lincoln placed upon kis next em- phatic speech. With this, he turned on his heel, and ran back to his spot of observation on the hill. The Englishman smiled, and strode away. Turning at a bend of the road, he saw the erect blue figure, rigid as he had first spied it, with the field-glasses to the eyes. Half an hour later, a thousand feet higher, and right among the mist, he found an inn at a point where four roads met—a curiously silent inn, with no open welcoming door. He pushed his way into an empty parlour, waking a sleeping landlord, who stared at him open-mouthed, with a loose, hanging lip. ,Lineoln called for beer. The landlord eyed him wonderingly, demanding Itow he catne there. "By the road, of course. I came from Otten- h6fen. '

The wirtli flung up his hands. " But the road is closed," he said. " The manoeuvres are on. Not a soul is allowed to pass. Did not the picket stop you? You Jee my house is empty. No ono travels to-day No one iu allowed to pass !" " Yet I am here," paid Lincoln, smiling, and a little amused. " Lusk at Ibis !" He drew his passport from his pocket, and unfolding it under the other'. rr'.. for.finirer on the red sixpenny stamp, then slid it to the signature, " Salisbury." "So —o—o !•' ejaculated the landlord, under- standing nothing of it, but believing that lie had to deal with an important personage. Si) he served his foaming beer as if he were waiting on a Prince. With narvinl directions as to the path, Lincoln started again op the narrow mountain track that led to the lonely heights where the Miimmelsee slept among its guardian pines. The mist lightened, and a watery gleam of sunshine pierced like an arrow shaft through its woolly thickness. Then the sun retreated again, and a drizzle began, changing quickly to pelting sheets of rain. Looking round for shelter, Lin- coln caw an overhanging rock, at the entrance to _narrow lateral track that led elVelf_ bele the

REV. GEORGE HARBOTTLE AT BEWSEY ROAD,

Sermon on the Welsh Revival.

1 The Rev. George Harbottle, of Dunstable, whose work lin the Warrington circuit some years ago gained for I him the cordial esteem of local Wesleyan Methodists, 1 I was the preacher at flewsey-road Wesleyan Church on 1 1 Sunday on the occasion of special services in connection 1 I with the Wesley Guild. Many friends of the rev. gentle- 1 I man availed themselves of the opportunity of hearing him again, and there was a large congregation at each service. In the evening Mr. Harbottle preached on ' "The Welsh revival," having recently visited South Wales to see something of the effects of the wonderful movement. He selected for his text the words -0 I Lord, revive Thy work." Habakuk 111., 2. and observed that the prayer of this ancient prophet had been repeated bv successive generations of Christian menand women. It now seemed as if God were answering the petition, certainly in Wales, and the revival movement which had commenced in Wales one hoped weuld spread ito England and, as Evan Roberts believed, would over- run the world. It might be of advantage to ask what 1 there was akin in the Welsh movement to the great revivals of the past, and what slifferentiated it from them. The origin of the movement was pretty well I known. A man who had given himself to prayer and who was deeply impressed and troubled at the failure of Christiantiy, became burdened in spirit. The word of God came home to him ; there was fire in his bones; and 1 he felt as if he had got a message from above. There I was nothing in that which had nat been seen before. A German monk who took his vocation more setio.usly than others received a special revelation from God • he began to preach with a new accent; his words were half battles and the Reformation was born and in a brief time spread over half Europe. The same happened in the case of the Evangelical revival. A few under- -graduates at Oxford gave themselves to prayer; were marked by their fellow-students for their earnestness, and nick-named Methodists. The people who liked things to be done "decently and in order" closed the pulpits of the Established Church to them, but they could not be silent. They preached in the open air with i" the heavens for a sounding board "and the Methodist 1 revival was born. The Welsh revival was like the old revivals in the simplicity of the preaching. There was an atmosphere in the movement which seemed to kill I the ordinary sermon. Arguments about the truth lof Christianity and the credibility of this or that i seemed entirely out of place and an offence. The gospel I which was preached in the revival was the simple Gospel of the love of God to all men, the sacrifice of the Cross, the possibility of instantaneous i conversion the possibility of a wen knowing that his i sins were forgiven, and the joyousness of deliverance ; from the thraldom of sin. Under tais kind of preach- ing people were converted in Wales as elsewhere, and

THE HUMAN H AND,

"CARMEN SYLVA "

(ELM. THE QUEEN OF ROUMANIA) (Author of" A Bounzanian Vendetta," V.).

The hand is the link between man and man it signs the pledge of every social bond, its synt holism plays an important part in all the lune. tions of public life—whether it be as the priest- ly hand, uplifted in solemn benediction, or a the silent witness to spoken words When raise on high to testify to/the sanctity of an oatl Clasped hands are the emblem of the &lima of nations as of individuals • the frank grasp the hand is the token of fuel friendship ; outstretched hand, the sign of welcome • the • conoiliation which puts an end to a bitter !ft long feud is attested by the simple action joining !hands. Man's • instinctive homage to woman is never better expressed than by t touch of his lips upon her hand,—the chivalrou deference to the whole sex is most fittingly sym- bolised in this simple , action. Ihe recognitio of its high import has caused numerous prover bial expressions relating to the hand to pas into our daily speech; to go through life "hen in hand " has become synonymous of the ita.• entire union in thought and deed ; we speak those who are unduly influenced by others ; having fallen " into their hands," and the es pression "to lend a hand" is equivalent to gi; Mg help in any form—not necessarily of a nia !null nature. Above all, the supreme beauty the hand—its instinctive abhorrence of the tout, that contaminates, of all base, unworthy tict„iw its shrinking from aught that might sully its native purity—is not all this admirably sintere. up inthe popular acceptation of " clean hands as the symbol of unstained honour ? This supreme significance of the hand in Kiehl intercourse has its counterpart in its important-, to the individual organism, when it serves as ; swift and sure medium of correspondence be. tween consciousness and the outer world. The physical evolution of the human race scarce re- cords an acquisition so valuable 'as that of sensitive and highly elaborated member, which has become—like speech itself—a vehicle for the rendering in concrete form of mental concep- tions, the dictates of the will, and the impres- sions of the senses. In the writteu characters it traces, thereby lending enduring form to the -hought of passing generations, in the figures it tnodels in clay or carves in imperishable marble. as in the pictures wherein by means of line al,. colour it fixes on the canvas fleeting scenes f the outer world or still fairer glimpses of the interior visioh,—in all these processes we per- ceive the band at work as the trained instru tnent—almost we might say as the conscious agent of the brain,—and the services it renders in giving objective life to the creations of the latter are indeed of incalculable worth. Nor are these services confined to the plastic arts alone,—though their action there is paramount and indispensable,—but music also, that most ethereal language of the human soul,—music itself would lose at least half of its significance And be shut out from the sphere of its most 7omplex development, had there not been be. ,towed upon us, in addition to the singing voice, the 'fingers, whose touch upon keys or strings has power to draw from their depths the hidden sweetness slumbering within. Man's noblest ideas must ,in course of time have perished, sterile and forgotten, had there been no means of recording them,—the highest flights of the :magination could never have taken shape, but for this interpreter. . . _

But it is above all in its untrained spontane- ms movements, ,in the direct and involuntary expression of strong and swift emotion, that the band's truest eloquence /lies. Races among whom the habit of gesticulation has died out can per- haps , hardly appreciate the full force of mean- ing to be conveyed in the graceful and appropri ate gestures of a pair of shapely hands. Just as :of expressive countenance may be 'likened to a mirror that reflects the hidden workings of the soul, so may every turn, every twist—nay, the merest quiver of the hand,—serve —more defi- nitely and more accurately even than the most mobile play of feature—as a revelation of each passing mood, each fleeting impulse or impreQ- ,ion. From the loving caress a light touch inf. to the fierce malediction invoked by the same fingers uplifted menacingly, is there any shade of feeling so subtle or so intense that it cannot be rendered in this mute language, which—unlike the forms of articulate speech -- is not restricted to any special branch of the human race, but is equally clear and compre- hensible to all? Can we mistake their purport, these convulsive signs of ,joy or sorrow, hope or fear,—the deprecatory or disdainful wave, the start of- surprise or nervous tremor of doubt, the rapid beating together of the palms hi acchuna. tion, the spasniOdic clenching of the fist in arger or despair ? Do they not tell their own tale, these hands, now outstretched •in eager entreaty, no thrown protectingly around some cherished object, or again silently wrung in agony, or clasped in. prayer? In their dumb show, defi- ance or scorn, wonder, horror, and remorse, are made visible to us, as it were by sel many lightning • flashes cast upon the sombre drama perpetually being enacted within the depths of the human soul. And although within the nar- row limits of our conventionalities, every strong outburst of natural feeling is so systematically repressed, that we mnst needs betake ourselves to more primitive peoples and a less artificial state cf society, in order to behold the picturesque display of uncontrollable and uncontrolled pas- sion, yet the artistic beauty and 'dramatic sin- cerity of such manifestations is amply testified to among us by the important development lent themes aids to oratory and the actor's art. Both at the tribune and on the stage appropriate ges- ture furnishes a sort of running accompaniment to speech, and much of their success who thus " shit the action to the word " is due to the persuasive power of this mute auxiliary. i Yet modern training is on the whole too much concerned with mere muscular development and too exclusively utilitarian in its tendencies to pay proper attention to the culture of this noble member; hence the finest specimens of grace and suppleness are chiefly to be found among those nations who have not yet learnt to con- sider material prosperity as the test of civilisa- tion, nor success in commercial enterprise as one of the principal factors in the same. For the beautiful slim hand, shapely and supple, we must go to the least flourishing of the Latin na- tions—the Spaniards notably, or to the Slays, the Gipsies even, and the handsomest of so- called savage tribes. Or better still, we must go back to ages that cherished and respected the nobility of the hand. It has been said that the armour worn by our ancestors in the Middle Ages affords conclusive proof of the smallness of the hands which wielded their heavy battleaxes and huge two-handled swords, and that scarce- ly in any instance would the iron gauntlet worn I by the Crusader allow entrance to his descend- ! ant's broader and clumaier hand. I think that I nowadays we pay far too little attention to the hand, in its way quite as interesting and as well worthy of study as the face, indeed often af- fording far surer indication of our thoughts. To a certain extent we can • control our faces, but how much less our hands, whose involuntary palpitations and pulsations not only betray—as thought readers " are well aware—the sensa- tions of the moment, but also give evidence of our habitual occupations. All hands have their own beauty and meaning, if rightly considered. The hand hardened by honest labour is as ad- mirable in its way as that which guides the pen or pencil, producing works destined to immor- tality. Sometimes though, innate vulgarity, successfully hidden under exterior polish in the rest of the person, is apparent in the hand alone,—sometimes even the tendency to crime. It is as if to hands like these evil clung like "the smell of the blood" that haunted the sleep of the guilty partner of Macbeth's usurped throne. .

Allusions to he supreme significance of the hand are frequent with the poets. Readers of Mrs. Igirowning's Sonnets cannot fail to recall that ffrst kiss imprinted by the lips of the poet- lover Camoens os "the fingers of this hand c!ili,e4witn I write ;" and tile Cainevere " as she swayed the rein w:th I.ainty finger-tips "is also it remembrance familiar to all. A hand like a white-wood ly;0,-solii sings another English poet in expisite while the whole dramatic action of perhaps :In. best known play of the great contemporary poet of Italy centres in the lovely hands of its lioi ine, the sculptor's wife. Oh I for the touch of a vanished band l" have all of us surely sighed at some inomellt of ol; lives. And who can think of hands, and filch' beauty and their charm, without calling to mind those that the genius of Vandyck has fixed int imperishable loveliness upon his canvas, each one of them so perfect, it would seem as if Art and Nature could go no further, the one in fur- nishing the model, the other in its pourtrayal. Heads such as these fitlY set oR in the lace

ruffles of those more relined and happier days, could belong to •none but high and noble na- tures ; the very soul of the niaormanimous, Cava- lier seems to speak ill them. That was the age of beautiful hands in Europe, and who can tell. I ask once more, how much we may have lost by the neglect that has succeeded the care and attention then bestowed on them? Alid do we value enough, or turn to sufficient account, the strange power of attraction, the magnetic forte known to emanate from certain hands, whose miraculous results have been attested at all periods and in all parts of the world,—the lay- ing on of hands, so practised in Apostolic ages, and for many centuries later, and now fallen into disuse ? _

, Who can deny that in some hands .mysterious power resides • a hypnotising force, that may be employed resides, good or. evil, and should surely often be turned to good use for, the healing of pain, for conjuring sleep ? How welcome is the gentle . touch that smooths our pillow in hours of pain and suffering, and how must the wounded and the sick ever bless the- -1,..nd and skilful hands that minister to them in tlizir need ! I was once watching the hands .ot- a great surgeon during a very difficult opera- tion, the dressing of a gunshot wound so .deep and so near to a great artery that the least slip might have proved fatal. The hands, although streaming with blood, so fascinated me with their. unerring skill cud dexterity, I could hardly take. my eyes off them, though I was busy adminis— tering chloroform to the patient, a poor yaung, wounded soldier, and, had to watch his face. very closely too, to observe its effect on him. And I thought to myself, there is something of the same pleasure in seeing these hands at work, that we experience in, watching the &Meals. handling of his • bow by the violinist, or the graceful action of a hand sweeping the strings of the harp. Nor can ever a woman's hands. however their beauty may be set forth by evers adjunct of the toilet, show to such advantage us when framed in, the coarse serge, sleeve of the Sister of Charity, whose soft touch charms away the anguish, of the deathbed she attends on, her heaven-sent mission. Each :one of these calls to aur minds the vision in the famous lines': Rose-bloom fell on her hands, together prest, And On her Silver cross soft amethyst, And on her hair a glory like a saint : She seemed a splendid angel, newly drest, Save wings, for heaven.",

No less beautiful in its way then this, the- picture of saintly hands crossed upon the bosom in prayer, is the noble attitude of classic anti- quity, the palms held up7fards outstretched to the Gods. . .

But the theme is so rich in its suggestions, these would lead us too far. .

It was while sitting 'at my typP-T,7iter that the perfection of the band as o !,I,xe of mec- hanism came upon me with redoubled force,— as I realised all the ingenuity and pains which man had expended in making an instrument after all so infinitely inferior to the correspond- ing instrument in the human frame. And I realised, too; for the first time, the incalculable speed of thought, in the appreciable fraction of time that intervenes in the transmission of that thought to the fo•ders from the brain. But the result of all rir musings was only as ever to fill me with more and more wonderment at the divine mystery of all creation, and more and more thankfulness for the blessings that in that Marvellous scheme have been so fully and free- ly bestowed upon Man.

Because Couldn't Digest Stronger Food..

"Mt GREATEST !IN' GOLF," BY HORACE

(

Mr. R. H. Albery delivered a lecture on "Wireless. telegraphy and Hertzian waves" at a meeting of the Literary and Philosophical Society held in the Masonic Hall, Bold-street on Monday evening under the presid- ency of Mr. F. G. Ruddock. The lecture proved highly interesting the first part being devoted to the investiga- tions which led up to wireless telegraphy. Mr. Albery pointed out that as soon as electric telegraphy was made possible and practicable by the aid of wires we began by trying to discard the use of wires. At first two wires were used, one for the direct current and the ' other for the return current. As early as 1838 it was found possible to do away with the second wire and use the earth itself for the return current, the earth being a good conductor. The idea of a wireless telegraph appeared to have presented itself to Professor Morse in 1842, when he telegraphed across rivers by arranging wires along the banks of a river so as to cause the water itself to conduct the electricity across. Step by step Mr. Albery led up to Signor Marconi's work, dealing also with the principles involved in the special apparatus he employed and which differentiated his system from all those that had hitherto occupied our attention. The lecturer contended that'to no one man alone was due the merit of having placed a new means of signalling at ;distance within our grasp, but rather that the intelligent application of isolated dis- coveries by many scientists had enabled Marconi to perfect the system now known as " Wireless tele- graphy." Marconi did not serve his apprenticeship in the school of misery as had so many famous men. He had ample means to pursue his electrical experiments after he left the University of Bologna. At his beautiful country home he worked quietly with a few crude instru- ments, signalling from one room to another. He then erected poles on his father's cstate, and sent signals. nearly two miles. in July, 1896, Marconi, then only 22 years of age, irrived in England from Italy, bringing with him a method of sending messages across space without intervening wires. The first trials in England were made between a room in the General Post Office,. London, and an impromptu station on a roof some 100 yards distant. In 1897 he covered two miles on Salis- bury Plain, and sent messages across the Bristol channel over a distance of nine miles. Other developments were traced to the year 1900 when Marconi interchanged, signals between England and Belgium over a distance of 100 miles, and it was mentioned that during thenaval manceuvres in the summer of 1900 war vessels " talked" over distances up to 166 miles. In 1903 the first wireless message from the United States to Europe was sent by Marconi from his wireless station at Wellfleet Massachu- setts to the station in Cornwall and was addressed to the. King. Manx performances- had already been accom- plished by wireless telegraphy, principally in connec- tion with shipping, and the army and navy had also been, experimenting with a balloon as receiver. Mr. Albery had thrown on the screen a number of diagrams which he had prepared and by means of which he explained at considerable length the method of transmitting messages across the ocean. The lantern was skilfully manipu- lated by Mr. R. H. Sutton.

On the motiOn-Of-thefiev. C. HARVEY CooK,seconded by Mr. J. FULTON SMITH, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Albery. . _

Meeting at Warrington.

On Saturday the annual meeting of the No. I Convention (South-West Lancashire) of the 1.0.0. T. was held in the Mersey-street Mission Room, Warring- ton, under the auspices of the "Temperance Mission" Lodge. Bro. J. Parr, C.C.T., of Leigh, presided over a very good attendance of representatives and visitors from Warrington, Earlestown, St. Helens, Widnes, Wigan, Hindley, Leigh and Ashton, and he was supported by all the executive officers, and Bro. R. W. Rycroft, D.E.S., and Sister Mrs. Duncan, N.C.V.P.,of Liverpool. Resolutions of sympathy were ordered to be sent to Bro. the Rev. J. A. Lee, D.C.T., of Liverpool, who is `seriously ill, and to Bro. J. Lindon, P.C.C.T., of St. Helens, on the illness of his wife. The CHAIRMAN, in his annual report, spoke very feelingly of the good work done by several of the lodges in the Convention area, and urged the members to still greater activity in the noble work of reclaiming the fallen and saving others from falling. Prospects of new lodges at Leigh and Sutton were bright, and he hoped to report these lodges in good working order at the next session. .

The CON. SECRETARY (Bro. E. Newman, Earlestown) reported that there had been good meetings of the Con- vention at various places during the past twelvemonths. Several lodges had changed their meeting places, and had greatly benefited thereby. There were 269 members in sight lodges, which was an increase of 33 members on the November quarter. Specially good work had been done by the "Nil Desperandum" (Wigan) and the "Temperance Mission" (Warrington) lodges, which showed increases of 15 and 11 members respectively. SISTER MRS. WOODS, C.S.J.T., of Earlestown, re- ported On the juvenile department. Although there was a decrease on the year, she was glad to state there was an increase of 71 members on the past three months. Much of the credit of this was due to the "Young Mission" Temple, Warrington, which showed an increase of GO members,mo re than doubling last quarter's returns.

The electoral work of the area was reported on by Bro. C. J. Snkarn.C.E.S., of Warrington. After refer- ring to the feeling of the country towards the present "Bung " Government, he said that the candidates for the Newton Division (Colonel Pilkington, ALP., and Mr. J. A. Seddon) had been written to by Bro. Bowler, E.S., of Ashton. Mr. Seddon was greatly in favour of temperance legislation, while the Colonel said he would "carefully consider the questions put to him." Bro. Sheath also criticised the attitude of the Warrington Corporation with regard to the George Hotel licence. Bro. J. WARBURTON, C. treasurer, of Earlestown, reported a very good balance in hand. The reports were adopted with thanks to the officers. The officers were then elected for the ensuing half- year as follows :—C.C.T., Bro. J. Parr Leigh (re- elected) ; C.V.T., Sister Mrs. Newcombe, Wigan; C. Councillor, Bro. G. Punshon, St. Helens ; C.E.S., Bro. C. J. Sheath, Warrington (unanimously re-elected) ; C.S.J.T., Sister Mrs. Woods, Earlestown (unanimously re-elected); C.Sec., Bro. E. Newman, Earlestown (re-elected): C. Treasurer, Bro.J. Warburton,Earlestown (unanimously re-elected);C.lVfarshal, Sister Miss Lowe, Wigan; C. Chaplain, Bro. Bowler, Ashton; P.C.C.T., Bro. Blundell, Wigan (re-elected); C. Guard, Bro. Lewis, sen., Widnes : C. Sentinel, Bro. Lewis, jun., Widnes (re-elected); C.A.5.,8r0. Newcombe,Wigan (re-elected), and C.D.M.. Sister Lambert, St. Helens. =The officers were installed by Bro. Rycroft, assisted by Bro. Wragg, of Wigan, and Sister Mrs. Duncan, Bro. Rycroft gave a brief address and a very enjoyable session was closed in the usual way. LOCAL HOCKEY MATCHES.

STOCKTON HEATH V. HUYTON.

These teams tried conclusions at Stockton Heath, in delightful weather. From the bully-off even play re- suited, with Huyton doing rather the more pressing. It was seen that the home backs were not in their best form. At length from a scramble in the circle Rogers managed to score the first goal for Huyton with a shot the home goalkeeper should have saved. After this reverse the home side played much better, and it was not long before R.Pierpoint equalized with a good shot. Half-time arrived with the score standing at one each and as the home side had been playing against the Slope it seemed as if Stockton would reverse the defeat sustained at Huyton earlier in the season. Upon the re-start Stockton at once pressed, but the Huyton de- fence was sound,and it was well on in the second half that S. Baxter put the home side ahead. After this Huyton played up strongly, and with the Stockton backs not playing too well, it looked as if their efforts Would be rewarded. Huyton then made a change in their forward line, Lewis taking up the inside right position and Rogers going centre-half. From the sub- seguent play it appeared that Huyton might have done this sooner,as just on time Lewis put in a hot shot that the home goalkeeper only managed to keep out of the net by giving a corner. Final—Stockton Heath 2, Huyton 1.

AN ADVENTUROUS CAREER.

Retired Seaman's Death from Blood Poisoning.

Mr. John Harrison, aged 75 years, a retired seaman, of 3, Birchdale, Appleton, died on Monday under some- what singular circumstances. It appears that twelve months ago he was travelling in an omnibus from War- ringtontto Stockton Heath and the conductor, whilst collecting the fares, accidentally trod upon one of his toes. Blood poisoning subsequently set in and, despite i medical attention, resulted n deceased's death. Mr. Harrison had had an interesting and adventur- ous carear. In early life he worked with the Swift Boats plying on the Bridgewater Canal between Manchester and Liverpool, and his nlevertiess as a horseman gained him quite a reputation. Joining the coasting service he had some trying experiences as a seaman. In 1896 his vessel was blown ashore near Fleetwood, and he and the mate had to cling to the rigging for five hours the heavy seas preventing the life boats from getting near enough to render assist- ance. On another occasion the craft on which he was engaged was run down in the Mersey near New Brighton and Mr. Harrison was immersed. Fortunately he was able to grasp a rope and was hauled aboard. , At Seacombe he had an experience of quite a different character, another seaman striking him senseless with a hammer and robbing him. The assailant decamped and was never discovered. About twelve months ago Mr. Harrison retired after an active life, one of the closing incidents of his career at sea being that he was washed ashore at Prestatyn. For twenty-five years Mr. Harrison had, when stsying at Appleton, been a worshipper at Hill Cliffe Baptist Church. THE INQUEST.

The inquest was conducted at the London Bridge Inn on Tuesday afternoon by Mr. Robert Dobson, coroner. Mr. H. J. Westbrook (Messrs. Robert Davies and Com- pany) appeared for the relatives of the deceased. Miss Elizabeth Harrison daughter of the deceased, said her father told her that on February 24th, 1904, he was travelling home from Warrington in an omnibus when the conductor, whilst collecting the fares, trod on one of the toes of his left foot. On reaching home he complained of pain, and for a week witness poulticed the toe. Dr. Parker was then called in and attended Mr. Harrison, with a short interval in the summer, until his death. Dr. Parker said that when he was called in the toe was contused and the nail severely crushed. There was, however, every hope that with proper treatment the inflammation would subside. A month or two later wit- ness discovered that there was evidently something which prevented healing, and he concluded that it was a piece of dead bone. Eventually two other toes were affected, and gangrene set in and eventually spread. Blood poisoning, resulting from gangrene, was the cause of death. He considered that had it not bran for the accident decaased would still have bran alive. Mr. Westbrook: In your opinion there is no doubt whatever that the cause of death was due dirc ..tly to the accident. Witness : That is so. The jury returned a verdict that deceased died from blood poisoning caused by the accident.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 18, 1905. WOMEN'S CHAT. IWOMAN'S REALM. SCIENCE NOTES AND GLEANINGS.

Sunday • pleasuring.—Religious revivals are just now much in evidence, and there is to be a new crusade, led by the Archbishops, against the week-end revels of the well-to-do. The motor car has done much to promote the secularism of the Sabbath, and week-end house parties and Sunday luncheon parties are now the general rule at country houses. 'lids, of course, means addi- tional labour for the hirelings, and domestic servants, ' though there is not perhaps much to be said On the point ' of their being overworked as they belong to classes , usually well able to take care of themselves. Sunday pleasures and amusements however have quite altered ideas about Sunday •as a day of rest. The modern ' system of living in flats is also conducive to Sunday pleasuring. People do net get attached to a fiat as to a , house. It is an abode, not a home, to be shut up and left at every opportunity for a week end visit, or other • change. The quiet Sunday has gone,and though the Arch. , bishops and other good men may attempt to stem the tide, it seems likely that we shall ultimat sly arrive at ' what is known as the Continental Sunday. I A Fixed Easter.—There has been some discussion as to whether we are going to keep Eastsr on the right day this year. According to the Prayer Book, Easter Day is alvray the first Sunday aftsr the full moon,which happens upon or next after the 21st March. In 1905, j the moon is full on that day, but the ecclesiastical , calendar is framed not on Greenwich time, but so as to , fix Easter on the same day all the world over. That is [ why Easter Day falls on April 23rd this year, instead of March 26th. From a holiday point of view, elate Easter is preferable,as it affords the prospect of better weather, but it would be more convenient if we could have a fixed Easter, and there would not appear to be any insuperable difficulties in the way of effecting such an alteration. The King and the Milkwomen.—The King has intervened, as it was expected he would, on behalf of the two old dames who had long kept a cake and milk stall in St. James's park. The business,if it may be so called, had been in the family for 300 years, and the two old women considered that they had acquired rights, which could not be taken away. The authorities how- ever required the removal of the stall in order to pro- , ccsd with the park 5-nprovements in connection with the Queen Victoria memorial in front of Buckingham . Palace, and as the two old dames would not go, their stalls were pull( 1 down and taken away. They had been threatened with the same fate some years ago, bu the late Queen came to their help, end it was therefore with some confidenc3 that they appsaled to the King in their distress. By his Majesty 'a desire they are to be given a new site, and two new stalls are to be put up for them, which they are to keep so long as they live. They are therefore now quite happy, and the splendid advertisement they have received will no doubt bring them a largely increased business. , The First Coue —The Royal Circle at the first Court was not a large one, Princess Victoria being only just convalescent, and by no means fit to attend State func- tions; the Duke and Duchess of Connaught, with the Princesses Margaret end Patricia, being on a visit to the Soudan ; and Princess Christian, accompanied by Princess Louise Augusts of Schlesung Holstsir having , gone to the South of France. The Duke and Duchess of4Connaught are returning home shortly,when all sorts of festivities are planned to take place at Bagshot and Clarence House. It is iamouredthat the engagement of both the Princess Margaret end the Princess Patricia will be araounced during the London S- :eon, and there is reason to believe that rumour speaks ti this time. Tall, slender, graceful girls, the Prince i are, with the most charming manners, end their mother's sweet expression. There is nothing dashing about either of them, they wear their heir in the neatest, plainest fashion, and dress as simply as possible. Dress at the Court.—Speaking generally, magnificence of fabric, and delicacy of color, were the points most sought after in dress,tor the first Court. White,as usual, was the invariable wear for, the debutantes, but it was little worn by those attending,or making presentations, undecided pastel shades being in such instances, evi- dently preferred Brocade and soft satin abounded, trains being either of velvet or those lovely shimmer- ing metallic tissues, which, this season have surely reached perfection. About Bouquets.—No new flower appeared in time for the first Court, and the orchid once more reigned suFreme. One reason for the extraordinary popularity this particular bloom enjoys is that it possesses very little or no perfume. The freesia was again much in evidenc 3, in the bouquets carried by debutantes, while malmaison carnations, and roses,were preferred by some women. Artists in flowers are excelling themselves this Iyear, and a comparison between the present day shower "arrangement of flowers and foliage carefully thought out, and designed with a view to its forming a part of a perfect toilette, and the old-time closely massed assortment of incongruous blooms with its little paper frill, is quite enough to make one rejoice that " the good old days " are indeed no more. How to keep Cut Flowers Fresh.-.-A piece of charcoal or a few grains of sal ammoniac in the water will greatly help to keep cut flowers fresh, the water being changed every day, and the stalks cut at the ends every other day. Flowers with stems full of sap, such as daffodils, narcissus, and other kinds, should have the stalks slightly slit before being arranged in the vases. The Pannier Again.—One renowned modiste, who has her headquarters in Paris, has been introducing the pannier upon a few of her evening gowns, but so far the idea has not "caught on" here. Panniers and the wasp waist go together, but of late years tbe great majority of our women have refused to lay up for themselves red noses, indigestion, and a host of other more terrible diseases by lacing themselves into corsets several sizes too small for them, and the stiffened pannier is not likely to find favour in these more enlightened days. Exaggeration is no longer tolerated, and the idea that the huge leg-o'-mutton sleeve will prevail during the summer months, is not likely to be realised. The full high-shouldered sleeve is the on:: ohe tolerated at the moment, but so far it has not reached grotesque propor- tions, and, save in the case of a few extremists, will probably not do so. The Theatre Hat Problem.—The obstructive 'theatre hat is happily much less seen than it used to be, and women now invariably remove their hats, or adopt some unobstructive headgear, specially suitable for theatre wear. Good sense ana better manners have prevailed where both coercion and cajolery have failed, and this problem which is almost as old as the theatre itself, would seem to be in a fair way of being settled. Such methods as requiring all the women to sit on one side of the house, as in Roman Catholic, and High Cherches, or putting up such notices as "all ladies over thirty years of age , may retain their hats," have never amended, but reform is involuntarily making headway, and it will be all the more sure since it comes of women's own accord, and more considerate fc sling for others. Ladies' Hockey.—Woman bee to sacrifice much of her queenly dignity and gra% in the hockey field, but in spite of that draw back, the game is becoming Increasingly popular with girls, and some of the County teams provide vety fine exhibitions of ladies' hockey. The Southern team have this year proved themselves the stronger having beaten the North, the West, and the Midlands, and they will provide nearly all the English eleven who are to play Scotland on March 11th at Edinburgh and Ireland four days later at Richmond. The match between Ireland Wales is arranged for March 17th at Swansea, and that between Ireland and Scotland ' will take place at Dublin at the end of the month. All these matches are likely to provide very keen contests, and the Southern team have every confidence that they will prove victorious in their meeting with Scotland.

MADGE.

The Congregational Sale of Work. The proceeds of the sale of work held in Cock- hedge Mission on Thursday, Friday, and a few hours on Saturday, amounted to £Bs.'

CHINESE MAGIC. Dr. Jules Regnault, writing of hygiene among the Chinese, treats of "fung-shui," the magic of directing genii or demons and favourable or unfavourable influences on men. The sorcerer, or Kan-fun-shui-ti, teaches the proper precautions to be taken in erecting tombs, houses, making roads, wells, canals, and so forth, in order to banish evil and invite good genii. They avoid straight lines and a northerly direction, because these are favour- able to bad spirits. Some of their knowledge is in accordance with hygiene and other sciences.

The spring flowers are so varied and lovely in their delicate colourings that they make the most attractive dinner-table decorations. PRETTY GIRL'S PROM

An example of a pretty little frock for a girl of t.n to twelve years of age. is shewn in the sketch. The chief feature of the dress is the smocking, which has again become very fashionable, especially for the trimming of children's dresses. The bodice is smocked at the back as well as in the front, and left to form a narrow frill where the bodice and lace yoke join. The epaulettes that fall over the sleeves are also. of lace, and are bordered with iribbon-velvet, and finished with a velvet bow on each side. The square at the neck is outlined with velvet. The sleeves are smocked at the wrist

and again at the shoulder, and the skirt is also treated in the same manner. This dress would look well carried out in a soft shade of reseda cash- mere, or it would make a simple party frock carried out in white washing silk, a narrow ruching of chiffon taking the place of the velvet.

FASHION GOSSIP.

' The modern craze for beads has resulted in neck- laces of opals strung together or with crystals alternating. These, according to the Tatter, are worn a great deal, opals being more in favour than ever, and next to amethysts are the stones of the year.

Flowers are very much worn in the hair this season, and roses take, of course, premier rank. The fancy for wreaths has, however, declined to a very great extebt, and instead of these clusters of two or three blossoms are fastened into the coils on either side.

Smooth straws, chips, and lace straws will be fashionable during the coming season. The colours most in vogue are blues of various descriptions— brown, butter colour, and once again the ever- popular and absolutely übiquitous champagne. Green is another colour of which the spring hat is to be fashioned, especially in conjunction with white and pale mauve lilac. The black hat, how- ever, is doomed to a great extent, and will be very little seen during the spring except for mourning. - Some of the prettiest of the new models are small toques fashioned of green tulle in rather a vivid shade, and trimmed with apple, pear, or cherry blossoms.

WALKING COSTUME.

A walking costume of dahlia-red cloth is shewn In our sketch. The short Eton coat is cut off to a decided point in the front, and is outlined with a stitched strap of its own material. The deep collar fits closely to the shoulders, and is trimmed with bands of satin of the same colour, inserted between two straps of cloth, the fronts being furnished with 'large red silk tassels. Satin is also used for the

smaller collar and for the wide belt which chews beneath the coat. Over the shoulders, it will be seen, are two shaped capes, and they are bordered with straps of cloth. The sleeves are gathered into a deep-pointed cuff at the wrist. The skirt is made to Zt closely over the hips, and cut in an ornamental fold towards the hem. The buttons used on the skirt and ccdlar are covered with the cloth. The hat worn with this costume is of black chenille, trimmed with insertion and a long white plume. _ _ _

WHO HAS THE BEST TIME?

All our affairs are settled for us nowadays by lady novelists. They deal with our social morality, they invent new reli,gions for us, and they decide sex problems of all kinds. We have just had an answer found to a question, presumably asked by scmeone, as to whether men or women have the best time; and as it would have provoked an obvious retort had the lady novelist who has elected to decide this matter declared that the balance was all in favour of man, we are, oi course, assured that the opposite is the case. I should say, comments "Ambrosia" in the World, that it is very much a question of class and of banking accounts. In the lower and middle classes a man unquestionably gets more out .of life than his wife. If a wrian of good social position has money, her/opportunities are about equal to those of the man. So far as treatment by the opposite sex goes, there is very little to choose •-ither way, lam inclined to think. A woman can ,tive heart-stabs that leave ugly scars, and a man can take all the colour from a woman's life. In this matter I do not think either has the "best time."

===== 0002601_19050218_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

CELERY AND CHICKEN SALAD. —This is an excellent method of utilising any scraps of white meat, such as veal, chicken, rabbit, &c., or of cold game. Take etual parts of crisp white celery and cold chicken; shred the former, after washing it, and cut the latter into small dice. Place in a salad- bowl, and pour over it a dressing made as follow: Mix together the yolk of a raw egg, a little salt, and one teaspoonful of mustard, add gradually one and a-half gill of salad oil, stirring all the time, and about half a gill of white-wine vinegar. Toss the chicken and celery thoroughly in this dressing, and garnish with hard-boiled egg, beetroot cut into fancy shapes, and the delicate leaves of the celery or sprigs of watercress.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
TREES AND RAINFALL.

Some persons ask if trees affect rainfall. That question is one on which foresters differ, but the large majority are satisfied they do induce rainfall. Vonrnulere, Schenck, and many others are emphatic in the claim that forests induce rainfall. It is certain they retain humidity to a great extent, and that alone would affect the rainfall; but the point on which all agree is the value of forests for the con- servation of water, preventing floods or drought, distributing the water evenly the whole year. It is a matter of history in foreign countries that when forests have been destroyed the rainfall has been greatly lessened and more spasmodic.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ARTISTIC MARVEL.

One of the greatest artistic marvels of the world is to be seen in the museum at Harvard University. This curiosity consists of hundreds of specimens of flowers and plants formed of glass, but with such exquisite fidelity to nature that they appear to be real, every tint and marking, every tiniest detail, being faithfully reproduced. They are made bya secret process, the artists being a father and son in Germany, who, it is said, may let their secret die with them. As an instance of the wonderful work- manship, it may be mentioned that the very hairs which appear on the stems on certain plants are reproduced on the glass imitatibns.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
GAS AND STEAM.

In a note on the relative advantages of the gas. engine and the steam-engine, the Engineering Review says: In certain branches of work the steam-engine can be applied with peculiar advantage, and the gas-engine cannot hope to rival it; in other branches, again, the positions are reversed, and the gas-engine is acknowledged to be superior or supreme; but, apart from those distinct realms where each type holds its own, there are many occasions for the installation of prime movers where the gas-engine and the steam-engine are in keen competition for supremacy, and a very close balancing of their relative advantages is required before a decision can be made.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
HEATING IN BOILERS.

Now that the relation of heating to surface in boilers attracts so much attention among engineers, some results of the use of the Serve ribbed tube on French lines are of interest. Owing to the internal ribs, they give off much more heat than ordinary tubes. M. Sauvage, the chief locomotive authority in France, has made a long series of practical tests, and finds that one ribbed tube is equivalent in evaporative efficiency to 2i smooth tubes. The heating surface is increased nearly 30 per cent, by the use of the ribs, and the efficiency increases at an even greater ratio. The cost has operated against more extensive use in this country.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
WIND SIGNALS ON RAILWAYS.

During the great gale on February 27th, 1903, a mail train was blown over on Levens Viaduct, on the Furness Line, and almost fell into the sea. The Furness Company, in order to prevent similar accidents, have now erected an ingenious instru- ment, which will automatically warn signalmen when there is unusual wind pressure. The apparatus consists of a combined wind pressure gauge and recorder, and is connected with an electric arrange- ment, by means of which bells are set in motion at distant signal-cabins. These bells will continue to ring so long as the velocity of the wind on the viaduct is dangerous to passing trains.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
BAUXITE BRICK.

According to the New York Iron Age, a patent has been issued to an Arkansas' inventor for making refractory brick out of bauxite, the ore from which aluminium is extracted. The value of bauxite as a refractory material has been known for several years, but its lack of cohesive qualities has heretofore prevented its being moulded into brick. This difficulty, it is claimed, has been overcome by a process now patented, thus giving to the steel trade a practicable and cheap brick for basic furnace lining to replace the expensive magnesite brick now so extensively used. The patent covers the use of a binder with calcined bauxite.

The natural bauxite used is particularly low in silica, and is bonded by a small percentage of plastic fire clay. The amount of silica in the finished brick is considerably less than 10 per cent., and is not appreciably detrimental to its elasticity. This brick is especially adapted for linings of basic open hearth furnaces. The highest grade bauxite bricks are used in the floor and walls up to the slag line, protected by a bed of calcined bauxite. Above the slag line cheaper brick, with a lower percentage of bauxite, may be used. The price at which this brick will be sold is considerably lower than that of mag,nesite brick, and there are one-third more bricks to the ton. They have been severely tested in several of the prircipal steel plants of the country, and these tests *re said to have proved very successful.

SUN AND "WEATHER.'

Mr. William Lockyer, Ph. D., writing in Knowledge and Scientific News, remarks that unfortunately one cannot go much further back than about fifty years when discussing the great majority of meteoro- logical observations, for in many cases they are either very sparse and broken, or it is not known with what degree of accuracy they were made. In the case of solar phenomena the investigator is still more restricted; for, although the observations of sun-spots have been made in a more or less crude manner for a great number of years, it was not till about the year 1830 that a systematic method of observation was adopted; further, the solar prominences, important indicators of the sun's activity, were only first recorded syste- matically in the year 1872. The reader will there- fore understand that before all these different phenomena can be correlated to enable,long-period forecasts to be successfully made, a greater period of time than the one at present available is abso- lutely necessary.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EQUATORIAL REGIONS.

This is, however, no reason why attempts should not now be made to find out whether these solar and terrestrial changes are related to each other, and if possible to point out how, from our present material, such a relationship, if detected, can assist us in making, at any rate, rough forecasts of approaching seasons. It is generally acknowledged that we are children of the sun, and life on this earth is only possible in consequence of his presence. Our sun is, so to speak, the fuel on which we are all dependent, and it is, therefore, quite natural to look to him as the instigator of our "weather." Now, our orange-shaped globe is surrounded by the atmosphere. The sun from without p'ours his rays down on the earth's surface and'heats it, whether it be water or land: this heated land and water warmS the atmosphere in contact with it, and this warmed air, which is now lighter than it was before, rises from the surface and is replaced by the cooler and heavier air flowing in at the bottom. In this way a current of air, a wind, is set up. The land or water most heated in this manner is that which lies in those regions,, over which the sun during a year passes overhead, and the reader will at once gather that this part of the world is that which includes the equatorial regions.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
VARIATION OF HEATING POWER.

It is well known not only in these but in all other latitudes that the "weather" is not the same every year. Sometimes there is a great abundance of rain, sometimes very little; one winter is very mild, while another is very cold. In fact, each continent has its own little meteorological worries, such as floods, droughts, famines, Ike. There seems little doubt that all these conditions are produced by changes in intensity or direction, or both, of the main currents in our atmosphere, and since these conditions depend for the greater part on the distribution of atmospheric pressure, it is this element which should receive the closest attention. It has been stated above that the most likely cause of these variations finds its origin in the sun, for, granting a change in his heating powers, the strength of the atmospheric currents, and consequently the atmospheric pressure, would be accordingly altered. The question therefore arises. Does the heating power of the sun vary? This is rlifficult to answer directly, although from certain observations of his surface the deduction is that grEat heat variations lo occur.

WARRINGTON MALE VOICE CHOIR.

Concert In Parr Hall.

The concert on Saturday evening, in which the forces of the Warrington Male Voice Choir and those of the Manchester Orpheus Male Glee Society were separately and jointly concerned, proved both a great success in attracting the public, and a convincing demonstration of the existence in our midst of much unsuspected material in the shape of capable men's voices. We gather from the printed programme that Mr. W. S. Nesbitt, the conductor of the Manchester Society is now closely associated with the present rapid advance- ment in musical proficiency of the Warrington Choir, and that the daring experiment of bringing the perform- ances of the two bodies of singers into what might seem to be sharp rivalry resolves itself rather into a friendly effort on the part of the older choir to encourage their younger confreres. It is meet there- fore that the Manchester vocalists should open the pro- gramme with an item such as last year's test piece of the Welsh National Eisteddfod. for in doing so a stan- dard of singing was set which was obviously worked up , to throughout an evening devoted to some excellent and I strenuous work. On two other occasions in the course I of the concert the Manchester Glee-singers placed en evidence their capacity to present with full effectiveness concerted compositions for male voices many of which have now come to be regarded as "classics." In . the chorus just mentioned and in " The Beleaguered," I ",When evening's twilight," and "Homeward bound," (the latter being this year's test piece at two principal Eisteddfods) ample scope was found for shewing that the Manchester Society possess all the essentials necessary to the making of an ideal choir. Although in "Sun of God "the tenor soloist was often too obvi- ously conscious of effort—a matter which marred its otherwise reposeful dignity—there was shewn here as in " Homeward bound 'a fine reserve of tone volume allied with a perfect command of its gradation. More- over there was alway a pointed attack and an emphasis of disjointed phrases and unison passages which were in every way admirable. The last mentioned chorus is unquestionably too long for sustained interest, albeit some sections of it, such as those descriptive of the lightning and the thunder,are so realistically picturesque as to stir the imagination to an unwonted degree. But as a test piece it bristles with just those multitudinous difficulties with which,as in this case, an ambitious choir finds pleasure in effectively grappling. The gem of the evening in choral music was undoubtedly Hatton's part- song, the bringing out of whose delicate nuances of expression and tone was both artistically and techni. cally particularly fine. An enthusiastic and discrimin- ating audience was not slow to appreciate the merits of this performance, its repetition in response to loud and continued applause being at once a graceful concession and a high compliment. The Warrington choir made its separate appearance after first singing in conjunc- tion with the Manchester contingent in "Martyrs of the arena." In dealing with the chorus "On the ram- parts" they had the advantage therefore of having become accustomed to their surroundings,of which how- ever they quickly proved they had little need,for under Mr. Hassall's conductorship, and with Mr. A. Trow at the piano, they sang with great steadiness and shewed a clear insight of the salient features of the piece. The supreme test of confidence is of course, only secured when in a piece like this the piano summary of the vocal parts is dispensed with. Yet sufficient points were scored to indicate that the Warrington choir is on the road to high achievements, and they are to be con- gratulated on evident progress recently made. "The Soldier's chorus" (Faust) by the combined choirs, at the beginning of the second part, was a rousing per- formance in which vocal units at the right moment revelled in "letting themselves go." So vigorous a rendering called forth such a storm of applause that clamours for its repetition could not be ignored. The singing of Sullivan's "The long day closes," also by the conbined choirs, was appropriately sombre and subdued and in an unostentatious though highly pleasing way concluded the choral singing. Miss Lucy Davies sang some songs in a sweet soprano voice and with an absence of effort which, while it failed to touch the depth of feeling which a due interpreta- tion of Tosti's "Good-bye" and "The Hindoo song" is capable of stirring, yet enabled her to impart a charm of her own to lighter vocal nsorccaux such as the coupled songs "Summer rain" (Willeby) and "The cuckoo" (Lehman). In response to a well-deserved recall, after the last mentioned. songs, Miss Davies essayed "Annie Laurie." with a similar easy naturalness which enabled her to do full justice to this old favourite. Mr. W. D. Holgate's voice and style are not of an altogether pleasing character, shortcomings which told most against him in "Lend me your aid ", a fine song to the exigencies of which he was scarcely equal. Blumenthal's "Evening song," on the other hand, he invested with so much tenderness as resulted in a more successful per- formance, suggesting the idea that in this vein of song his forte really lies. The violin solos by Mr. Harry Whitfield were very uneven in the unexpected way in which they evidenced both good and bad technique. The tone on the 0 string especially was poor, while the double-stopping in the upper reaches of the instrument was sometimes wonderfully sweet and true. So also the bowing and fingering in the "Witch's dance" often fell far short of that perfect delicacy and precision which are absolutely necessary for its satisfactory per- formance. The "Air vane" (Contiu) was perhaps the best of Mr. Whitfield's solos, all of which it should in fairness be noted, an indulgent audience readily applauded. It is not because Mr. Thomley-Dodge's contributions as the humorist were little meritorious that reference to them is made at the conclusion of this notice. On the contrary they were greatly superior to much that Warrington has had to content itself with in this line. The clever original sketch of a modern pantomime, the really humorous encore song "Has it over occurred to you?" and the genuinely pathetic "Baby Bill" are enumerated to indicate the range of Mr. Dodge's versatile gifts as a society entertainer as he would be more fitly designated. In all the things that Mr. Dodge did so successfully, there was a refreshing spontaneity and a freedom from vulgarity which made it a real pleasure to listen to him and secured for him a ready welcome from the audience. Mr. E. R. North's piano accompaniments were excep- tionally well played.
