JOHN WALKER & CO. EXAMINER OFFICE,

Mr. ASQUITH moved THE NATION'S DE HAND his Diss ol utio n AND MR. BALFOUR'S amenurnent on Wed- ANSWER. nesday, and was met by an express- sion of the Ministerial readiness to take the verdict of the House. It is not the verdict of the House that is needed, but the verdict of the con- stituents. Mr. BALFOUR has been talking of his blessed fiscal formula for eighteen months. Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBERLAIN talked upon it on Wednes- day, and taunted the Opposition with their adhesion to the stale old dogmas of sixty years ago But the country wants the opportunity of reiter- ating its belief in those dogmas, and Mr. BALvoun will not give it. He takes his stand upon the opinion expressed by Mr. GLADSTONE in a letter to Lord GRANVILLE, that it is unconstitutional to dissolve in deference to bye-elections. In his letter to the QUEEN- in 1874, Mr. GLADSTONE undoubtedly assigned the unfavourable bye-elections amongst his reasons for a Dissolution. There is no funda- mental difference between the two judgments. in 1874 Mr. GLADSTONE had to reckon not only with the bye-elections, but also with the disrupted tendency in his own party. All the symptoms pointed to a great weakening of his author- ity. This is precisely the case with the present Government. Mr. BALFOUR cannot pretend that his Ministry is the Ministry to which the country gave its con- fidence in 1900. The significance of the bye-elec- tions is that they have gone against a new Minis• try, the ministry of the blessed formula which Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBERLAIN glibly repeated in Wed- nesday's debate. "Let us regain our commercial freedom of negotiation with other Powers," says the Chancellor of the Exchequer, who denies to the country the freedom of passing judgment on this policy without further delay. This is the only freedom which is of any importance in this affair ; but Mr. BALFOUR is so far from recognising it that he is apparently contemplating, not only one Session more of his remarkable Ministry, but two Sessions. The Redistribution Bill will not be pro- duced this year, but a Boundary Commission may be appointed to report on the survey of areas, and this bland and unabashed Cabinet may delay its scheme until 1906.

This is the policy of the Minister, who will not answer " Yes "or "No " to the plain question whether he agrees in principle with Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN. To cite Mr. GLADSTONE'S authority for such a defiance of public opinion is the master- stroke of Mr. BALFOUR'S assurance. We can I imagine what Mr. Gladstone would have said to a situation which is entirely without precedent it: our political history. But Mr. BALFOUR is evi- dently confident that he can carry on with a suffi- cient majority an d keep the restive supporters of Mr. . CHAMBERLAIN in check. It is notorious that they are eager for a General Election ; but if their leader is willing to play Mr. BALFOUR'S game, he must be content to suffer a considerable loss of prestige. He can scarcely keep up his fiscal campaign for another year or eighteen months. His recent speeches have been stale and ineffective enough. In what condition is his propaganda likely to find itself by 1906? That Mr. BALFOUR'S fiscal policy is practically identical with Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S is evi lent ; but he does not take it 'very much to heart, and might not be sorry to see it quite forgotten by the Autumn of next year. They must always be in the hope that in the course of two more years something may turn up to 'divert public attention to more exciting topics. Should the bye-eiections in the course of that period pursue their steadily-adverse tendency and reduce the Government majority by one half, Mr. BALFOUR may cheerfully remind us that with a majority of only forty Mr. GLADSTONE !Let the Patliament of- 1892. Never very sensitive to I public feeling, the Prime Minister seems now to have adopted the maxim that the country must be deliberately flouted tor its own good. It has the supremely bad taste to be sick of this Government, and must be made to understand that Mr. BALFOUR will postpone to the last possible moment the only constitutional means by which it can turn him out. The penalty of this sublime Statecraft may be delayed, but it will be severe when it does come.

Our Conservative friends must have been hard- hit by the immense success and unbounded enthusiasm of the recent Lloyd George meeting. The official organ of the party in Warrington, evi- dently under orders laboriously attempted on Saturday to write Lloyd-Gaorge down. They devoted over a column to a criticism of his speech, which is quite the most excellent testimony we have yet had of the weightinsss of that oration. Evidently the Warrington Conservatives cannot afford to treat Mr. Lloyd-George lightly, any more than his great antagonist, Mr. Chamberlain, can. Even Liberals will, we are sure, be vastly amused at this belated I attempt to extract the sting from the Welsh Champion's criticism of Protection. The damage has been done, and not even the " Observer " can repair it. They might as well cultivate some of Mr. Balfour's philosophic calm for the last blow has not been dealt yet-, by any means. Mr. Winston Churchill and Mr. John Burns will very likely follow in Lloyd-George's footsteps, and also bay things which will be unpleasant for Tory hearing, and as difficult for the " Observer" to explain away.

At Monday's meeting of the Edu cation Corn rnittee, the estimated receipts and expenditure o the various sub-committees for the ensuing twelve months will be submitted. On these the rate for educational purposes in the borough will have to be based, and it is reassuring on glancing at the figures to find that the deficit which has to be made up out of the public funds is not largely in excess of last year's. Rumour has, as usual, been busy in the town of late, and an extra 3d. over and above the existing lid, rate, has been freely prophesied. The estimates show that the increase will not involve more than half that sum, even when a possible grant of £3OO to the Grammar school, and other contingencies, are allowed for. The education department and its responsible head, Mr. J. Moore Murray, are we think, entitled tip some congratula- tion for having so well combined efficiency with economy. Warrington is not heavily-rated for Educational purposes is comparison with many towns, and if the committee can get over the accommodation difficulty in a manner satisfactory not only to themselves but to the Board of Edu- cation, the ratepayers will have little excuse for the growl which comes so readily to their lips when faced with the periodical dernand-note.

Local Free Churchmen have this wesk been in annual gathering assembled, and they had the , pleasure of listening to an admirable address from the Rev. J. G. Greenough, M.A., an ex-president or the National Free Church Council. Both Mr. 1 Greenough and the chairman, Mr. Charles E. Park.3r, alluded at length to the Education Bill and its disabilities affecting Nonconformists. There is no topic, to-day, in which Free Ohurchmen take a keener or more intelligent interest, and it is to their credit that they boldly face the difficulties of the situation and attempt to formulate remedies. Mr. Parker, in the short time at his disposal, gave one of those thoughtful contributions to the question which emphasize his value as President of the Warrington Free Church Council. He did not devote any time to belabouring the Govern., ment but entered at once into the problems which trouble the mind and vex the spirit of all Nonconformists. "To his mind," he said, "to harmonise the interests of parent, church and state (and when he spoke of church he meant all denominations) in a national system of education was a practical impossibility.' This is the crux of the whole difficulty, and the principle involved will have to be generally accepted before the problem of religious instruction can be solved in a way likely to satisfy the great majority of people, and particularly thoss outside the Anglican ' Church. "If the ground were cleared in this way, he did not think it would be difficult for the State, and the parents who formed the State, to agree as to those things in which a child should be edu- cated." The matter would, in our view, resolve itself into a problem of comparative simplicity once the principle was accepted that the churches need not concern themselves directly in the teaching of the children. The elementary instruction which is given to-day forms common ground for agree- ment, and we see no reason why a certain course of moral or bible teaching should not be generally accepted as an inseparable adjunct to the training of the young in the day schools, quite apart from any denominational dogma. With all the rate- supported schools owned by the State, and under 1 public control, the bugbears of sectarian teaching, and denominational tests for teachers, would I vanish like noxious vapours before the breeze. The ideal to aim for is simple and quite within the possibility of realization.

The individual who is apt to judge the work of the Borough Police Force by the number of prose- cutions which he reads of in the newspapers can have but a very poor conception of the multifarious duties devolving upon the custodians of the peace. There has beet' issued this week the annual report of the Chief Constable, and it is, perhaps, chiefly interesting by reason of the insight whichit gives into the important and widely varied work under- taken by the force. It is no longer a case of deal- ing solely with " crime and criminals," although that of course is the great purpose of the maintenance and equipment of a smart body of men. One of the newest departments to claim attention is that opened for the registration of motor•vehicles and drivers, and it is pleasing to hear that, with the co-operation of I the public, the work has proceeded smoothly and efficiently. Since the enforcement of the new Act the control of street trading by children has also become a very responsible task, an during the year 166 licences have been granted to youngsters who trade in our thoroughfares. Mr. Talbot has a special word about the excellent effect of the licecsing and registration of child hawkers, for he points out that the boys are better disciplined and "the police are enabled to distinguish between those who follow street tra:ling as a genuine means of earning money for desery ing parents, and young loafers who .make it an excuse for street prowling." It is gratifying to learn that the impressions loaned by H.M. Inspector on the occasion of his annual inspection of the force and buildings were entirely satisfac- tory and the Chief Constable himself has nothing but praise for the high standard of conduct of:the men under his command.

As to the state of critne in the borou,gh,tlota Chief Constable reports a decrease in the convictions and committals for the more serious, i.e. the indictable offences, of 13. On the other hand the convic • tions for non-indictable offences exceed last year's figures by 133, so that there is still plenty of room for improvement. The epidemics of robbery which periodically occur do not seem to affect the peace of mind of many people, if we may judge by the absence of precautionary measures. It is indeed gurprising fact that the police on night duty have found 685 shops, houses,and warehouses open or insecure, and notified the occupiers. The same occupiers would doubtless have been amazed, and inclined to blame the police, had advantage been taken of their negligence or absent-mindedness to relieve them of property I We fear the day is still far distant when bolts and bars may be discarded andimplicit trust be placed in one's fellow men.

Public interest is growing in the development of the motor omnibus service Those provincial towns are watching London's experiment which are not themselves prepared to load the way. But the motot omnibus is already familiar in our coun- ties and the:more enterprising industrial centres. There is hardly a railway company that is not to-day reaping the substantial advantage of their employment on rural highways to supplemmt an otherwise inconvenient railway service. Thu most sensational news, perhaps, is that Ino fewer than 500 motor omnibuses may he running in London oefore the year is out, for thirteen or fourteen leading companies have placed considerable orders. The most recent improve- ments in construction largely obviate noise and other inconveniences of which something was once heard. The cars travel smoothly, pick their way through traffic with ease, and are amenable to all the controls which are essential in crowded streets. But there is one drawback which does not seem to have been considered, and that is the danger of side

Along the country roads it has conceivably a useful sphere of action. Here it is likely to •be the railway company's effective reply to the damaging tramway competition of suburban places. Its interior furnishing is the last word on the comfort of travelling. The Kaiser,for instance, has a sumptuous saloon, with table and regal upholstery. On the other hand, village travellers may shortly see the carriers' carts which have laboured up the hill roads for generations re- placed by a swift locomotion which will make the remotest hamlet seem to the small holder no more than a suburb of that Mecca of the countryside,the market town.

It has been complained by Colonials that, although the Mother Country is ready enough to acknowledge the bonds which unite her to the Colonies, her people really do not understand the point of view of their over-sea relatives. The defect is common. Tne temperaments of people at home and people in the Colorlies are different— a fact due very largely to environment, and things which appear in the natural course to the one are viewed as remark- able and incomprehensible by the other. It is impossible, for instance, for the Colonial to understand the ways of red tape which the Englishman accepts as Dart of the rule of life. A correspondent of one of the London papers has been moralising on Canadian loyalty. It is good to have assurances on the point, though there has been no reason to doubt the loyalty of the bulk of the inhabitants of the Dominion. English people will never forget the loyalty of the contingents of Canadian Volunteers who rendered such service in the Boer War. The Colony has proved by sacrifice her place in the Empire, and her services have been generously recognised. What is wanted now is perhaps a little more recognition on either side of the difference in the points of view which exist —a little concession to the prejudices of youth and of age. Tnere is no real lack of sympathy or appreciation here, and Colonial visitors who think there is have failed to probe the real sentiment of the English race. We are not demonstrative, but our feeling of gratitude and pride in the sons of the Empire is deem

Death of Mrs. James Kenwort hy Many of our readers will regret to hear that Mrs. Mary Kenworthy, wife of Mr. James Kenworthy, timber merchant, Dunmore, Grappenhall, passed away on Tuesday. The deceased lady, who had lived in the district all her life, had of late suffered from influenza and congestion of the lungs, but heart failure was the cause of death. When health per- mitted, she attended Wycliffe Church where her husband for many years held the office of deacon.— The funeral took place at Hill Chile graveyard yester- day afternoon in the presence of numerous relatives and friends. The Rev. John Yonge officiated and was assisted by the Rev. Albert Walker, of Bradford. Amongst those present were :—Mr. James Kenworthy (widower), the Rev. The Rev. J. F. Matthews gave an address at the weekly meeting of the Warrington P.S.A. Society on Sunday his subject being "When a man's down." He , li contended that the "out of work" cry in the town was not an exaggerated one, the state of the poverty being chronic, and in many instances men and women in search of work were not really fit to fulfil any task for want of food. He said that if his purse ' was deep enough he could spend £lOO a week on needy cases, but his means would not allow this. He did not forget that all were brethren,whether rich or poor, and he realized that it was the duty of all Christians to help one another as far as lay in their power. The spirit of the age was when a man's down keep him down, get of him, and this in a professed Christian country -, but true Christianity taught us to help such as are in distre es, and not trample on them. He admitted that some people hardly deserved consideration at first thought, but had they had the chances given them to bring out the true nature that was in them ?—The meeting was well. attended, Mr. Downes presiding. Mrs. Williams sang with excellent taste "Light in darkness" and "Even me," being very ably accom- panied by Mr. B. Wadsworth.

A Warrington Rag Gatherer in Trouble. At the Prescot Police Court on Monday, a Warring- fon rag gatherer, named Ralph Taylor was brought up in custody charged with stealing a horse, cart and iset of harness, valued at -5,12, the property of Thomas Clarke, marine store proprietor, of Prescot and St. Helens. The evidence for the prosecution showed that on the Bth inst. prisoner hired Clarke's horse and cart for 2s. per ',day, the agreement being that he should return them at the end of each day. Nothing further was heard of the horse and cart until they were found in prosecutor's yard at St. Helens on Saturday. Prisoner was arrested in Warrington on the same day. He pleaded that he had not stolen the property. It was merely a breach of contract. He was remanded in custody for seven days. Lecture on Charles Kingsley. The Rev. Trevor H. Davies, of Manchester, lectured before a good congregation in Padgate Wesleyan Chapel on Monday evening on "Charles Kingsley, Knight of the Nineteenth Century." The Rev. Joseph Manning presided. The lecturer said that Kingsley, like other great men who had left impres- sions upon English history, commenced his service in solitary prayer before the altar of consecration. No man in the nineteenth century had a more practical conception of religious life than he had, and it was Kingsley, also, who was responsible for a large share of the practical, social, and philan- thropie Christianity in which they in England rejoiced so much at the present time. He was a scholastically educated man and was a great democratic leader and a m )st true and perfect knight. He was always ready to do the duty that lay before him. Justice and mercy, and that rigid self control which kept hirri from speaking a hasty word or harbouring a mean suspicion, combined with a divine tenderness, were the govern- ing principles in his home life. (Applause.) On the motion of Alderman Bennett, seconded by Mr. Richardson, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Davies. "PREMIER" CYCLES at 7 guineas startling value.— J. S. Am for CIINNHIGHAM'S HIGH-CLASS NinsTERAL WATERS.—Advt. - A Successful Sewage Manure. Convincing evidence of the efficacy and value of Native Guano, a sewage manure prepared by the -Native Guano Co., Ltd., New Bridge-street, black- friars, London, E.C. is forthcoming in a further batch of reports received ' from farmers, gardeners, nurserymen and others who have used the manure in their fields and gardens. The writers unanimously testify to the fertilizing properties of 'Guano which they have employed foT almost every (kind of crop. The manure, it may be stated, is ex- tracted from town sewage and contains the well-known valuable properties of cesspool matter. Its effects, both immediate and lasting, show it to be 'the natural manure for the soil, returning , . • what has been taken from it The company, whose works are at Kingston, Surrey, consider it a source of regret that the sewage from which guano is obtained should continue to be neglected, wasted, and allowed to become a source of pollution to rivers and streams. The directors invite the assistance and co-operation of landowners, far..zers aGnud: noco.'stop ( others, process inine xtteerLd. ionwgnthnee neighbourhood, ootfiteo c Native thus • subserving not only their own direct interests but also the welfare of the community at large. The general adoption of this system for the treatment of sewage would, it is contended, not only present an L .economical and efficient solution of sewage disposal, but enable what is now a nuisance to towns, to become a source of profit to the country generally% - .and especially to farmers,who would have a valuable , ' ,manure produced almost at their own doors.

Personal. Our readers will be pleased to hear of the success of Mr. Walter G. Longdin, son of the Borough Sur- veyor and first assistant in his department, who has been appointed assistant civil engineer to the munici- pality of Rangoon (India)—a place about three times the size of Warrington. Mr. Longdin. jun., has been in the Surveyor's Department since 1.89, having served his articles with his father. At the meeting of the Paving and Sewerage Committee on Monday, the Chairman (Councillor Tinnion) congratulated Mr. Longdin on his success, and wished him good luck in his career. Ur. Longdin sails next week to take up his new duties.

Suicide at Warrington. George Edge, 47, Marbury-street, aged 36, a fitter, committed suicide by hanging himself on Friday morning. His son, Thomas Percy Edge, on coming downstairs saw his father hanging by a rope at the bottom of the stairs. He got the assistance of a carter named Morris, and the body was cut down, but life was extinct.

Progressive Labour League. "Trade Unionism" was the subject under discus- sion at the weekly Meeting of this League held at the Liberal Club on Tuesday evening. On opening the debate Mr. J. Elwell said that trade unionism was a combination of workmen to secure the most favour- able conditions for labour upon the general body of its members. Its object was to give the labourer the advantage and to foim the rank and file of the industrial armies into organization able to deal on something like equal terms with the employers. Trade unions had done and were doing a good work, and they were determined that that good work should go on. One of the best results of the associations amongst workmen had been the mental culture. They had promoted the betterment of the conditions under which they laboured, shortening of howl of labour and increasing the wages, self protection against sickness and old age, and provision for decent interment. An interesting discussion followed and Mr. Ellwell was thanked for his paper. UNDER THE CLOCK for Good B(X)TS.—Advt.

Successful Ambulance Team. Congratulations are due and will be readily accord to the Warrington Central Station ambulance team for their highly creditable victory in the annual competi- tion in connection with the Cheshire Lines Committee which was held at Liverpool. Eleven teams entered, the Warrington Engineers as well as the station squad taking part. The result was that the Warring- ton Station corps carried off the first prize of a cup with an electro-plate tea and coffee service for each member, the points awarded being 29(1, out of a possible 300. Manchester Central obtained 224 points and Huskisson Goods, 219 points. The following are the members of the Warrington Central team :—Messrs. H. T. Williams (leader), J. Fogg, H. Holloway, James Blake, J. T. Robertson and R. Nutter (reserve). The team have competed upon no fewer than eleven occasions, and have been awarded eight firsts,None second, and tvvo third prizes, and have also won the Warrington and District Shield upon two occasions. They have com• Peted in the National Railway Ambulance Challenge bhield Competition in London upon four occasions,and I have been placed three times fifth and once third, the latter being in May against 23 entries.—At the distribution of prizes, Mr, James Pinion (general manager) presided and in the course of his address said that the first time a squad representing, the C.L.C. competed for the St. John Ambulance Society's shield was in 1899. They could then get no nearer than the fifth place. The highest position they had secured was when the Warrington team last year reached the third place, and as they would have to compete again it was hoped that they would come out on top. (Applause.) The prizes were distributed by Mrs. Pinion.—The Warrington team will again go to , London in April next to compete for the St. John's ' Ambulance Association Railway Ambulance Chal- lenge Shield, which is open to all railway companies in England and Wales.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 18, 1905

THE SUGAR CONVENTION.

Conservative Labour Party's Views.

THE RECENT LETTERS. A meeting of the Conservative Labour Party was held in the St. Barnabas' schoolroom on Thursdaynight. There was an attendance of about 100 people, many of whom dissented from the views of the speakers. Mr. J. Hands presided and was supported by Messrs. F. Fearneley, B. Raymond, R. G. McCartney (Manchester.)

===== 0002601_19050218_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
THR TANNING INDUSTRY.

- ....... The CHAIRMAN said that after the last meeting of the party, a gentlemen stopped him with respect to the statement he made at the meeting with regard to the tanning business and other industries which he had men- tioned. That gentleman told him that while he agreed with him about other trades he did not do so with regard to the tanning business. What he (the Chairman) said at the last meeting was that they were gradually but surely losing- the tanning business chiefly through American competition. The gentleman in question told him that the trade in tanning was no better than it was 20 years .ago. Since then he had spoken to Mr. Reynolds of Mersey-street tannery, and -Mr. Atkin of the Tanners Lane Tannery Co., and the latter told him that he was perfectly correct in his statement, and that the tkade was affected all over the country owing to foreign competition. THE SUGAR CONVENTION.

Mr. FEARNELEY said he had seen a couple of letters which appeared to be in reply to some remarks made by Mr. Pierpoint, M.P., to the effect that the increased price of sugar was not due to the Convention. That was going to be his text that night. He could not take his text from a more clear-headed man thon the mem- ber for Warrington. ( Applause.) He would prove by giving the whole facts that the position which the Liberals took up was absolutely inconsistent with the professed principle of free trade. Mr. Gladstone twice over declared that those bounties were an improper method of trading and he endeavoured to have them removed. Sir William Harcourt also de- clared the bounties to be an immoral method of trading. Even Sir Henry Campbell Banner- man had declared exactly the same thing—that the system of bounties in connection with the sugar trade was immoral and improper and they ought to make an endeavour to make France, Austria, and other countries remove sucht:fan unhealthy method of trading. Prior to 1870 the sugar trade so far as this country was concerned was chiefly in cane sugar from the West Indies. The beet sugar trade so far as this country was concerned was then in its infancy, and to encourage this trade, France, Germany and Austria gave a small bounty to it. Mr. Chamberlain at that time called atten- tion to it,. and wished this country to endeavour to remove that bounty, but though Mr. Gladstone and his Government were against it, they did not think it worth While then. Since that time the bounties had increased enormously. Now it was recognised thatbounties were the very worst form of Protection, and it was utterly inconsistent of their Liberal friends to act as they had done in regard to the question. By the competi- tion of bounty fed sugar with the cane sugar, they had increased the number of sugar relining works in Germany, France and Austria, at the expense of this country. Moreover the patent laws in Germany were much more favourable to the patentees than they were in this country, and between the two the sugar refining industry in this country had almost been , killed. The bounties were instituted for the purpose of , driving out the competition of cane sugar so that nothing but beet sugar should have a chance, and of course in a very few years this country would have been at the mercy of the beet root growing countries. They were told that the price of sugar had gone up very largely in consequence of the sugar convention and the tax upon sugar. He was going to allow that the tax upon sugar was bound to have an effect upon the price. It necessarily would inctease the price, but that was their share in the cost of the recent war which had to be paid for. He had no doubt they all agreed that the war was necessary. (A voice: "No!") At any rate the tax on sugar might be taken off by the coming budget or the next after that. It was purely an ephe- meral.matter. Now, with regard to the price, he would show that that was not affected by the Convention. During the past year there was a shortage in beet owing to the dry weather. The law of supply and demand of course caused the price to go up. But by the signing of the Convention, a change had been brought about in the West Indies. As soon as the West Indies found that they were to have a fair field, they woke up, and the consequence was that after tasingle year's operation of the Convention, we got from them an increase of 447,000 tons. This reduced the shortage to 830,000 tons. If, however, the Convention had not been signed, the shortage would have been 1,180,000 tons. He must remind them that they were not giving the West Indies anything except fair play, and he contended that this was to the interests of the whole Empire, (Applause). At the close of the:meeting a number of questions were asked. _

Are you, asked a member of the audience,prepared to debate the Chinese Labour qumstion and the taxation of sugar with Alex Grimshaw of the I.L.P. ?—Yes,replied Mr. Fearnley—l am prepared to debate with anybody. TelllMr. Hayes when you desire the debate and I will be there to debate. (Applause.) Are not, asked another, the whole of the Co-operative Society against the Sugar Convention ?—That is not so. Are not the whole of the Trade Unionists of the day against it ?—I cannot deny that because I don't know. I cannot claim to know all the Trade Unionist officials like some people seem to do. (Laughter.) Are not the jam, confectionery and biscuit trades badly hit, owing to the Convention ?—I have shown in my address that the price of sugar has not been risen by the Convention so that therefore it cannot have affected them:

A number of questions were also asked regarding the Chinese labour question, Mr. Fearneley in reply stating that it was the wish of the Transvaal people that this labour should be introduced and he denied that it inter- fered with white labour. The meeting then terminated.

INDEPENDENT LABOUR PARTY. Its Attitude Locally at the Next Election.

In connection with the local branch of the Independent Labour Party a well-attended public meet- ing was held in Brick-street schools on Wednesday evening. Mr. William Knowles presided and the prin- cipal speakers were Mr. J. A. Seddon, Labour candidate for the Newton Division, and Councillor Fred Stott. The CHAIRMAN said that was the first meetibg held by the party in that part' of the town, and they intended to hold several in different neighbourhoods. The District in which they were met that night was in the Warrington polling district of the Newton Division, and he hoped that all Socialists and trade unionists would do their utmost to assist Mr. Seddon in his candidature. He contended that the contest was one of "money bags versus brains," and he hoped brains would be at the head of the poll. (Applause.) He wished to divide the wheat from the chaff in regard to local labour matters. Two other associations were now bringing themselves to the front —the Conservative Labour Party and the Progressive Labour League. The wheat consisted of the, Indepen- dent Labour Party, Trade Unionists, and Socialists, whereas the other bodies he had named were simply chaff. The Chairman related an amusing incident concerning the recent candidature of Mr. Frank Fearneley, of the Conservative Labour Party, in a municipal contest. Having noticed the good effect of the initials 1.L.P., Mr. Fearneley decided to adopt a similar plan and call his party the Conservative Labour Party. At a meeting which he addressed he announced his party as the Conservative Labour Party, whereupon a sharp-witted mem bar of the audience exclaimed " that will do. Chinese Labour Party !" (Laughter). Mr. Knowles concluded by advocating the nationalization of railways and the abolition of mining royalties. Councillor Srorr, after giving a short history of the movement in Warrington, said that politics would have no interest for him at the next general election as far as Warrington was concerned,having.regard to the fact that there was no L tbour candid ite. ill therefore intended to devote all his energies to the Newton Division, and his advice to socialists, trade unionists, and all who believed in the Labour movement was to keep clear of Warrington politics and to do all they possibly could to return Mr. Seddon. He had one of the greatest opponents of trade unionism in the House of Commons to face, and they hoped he would achieve a great victory. The labour movement had made great progress in War- rington, and they had now four members on public bodies. Mr. J. A. SEDDON gave an interesting address on "The hopes, aims and ideas of the new Labour Party." Fie condemned Protection as, in his view, it was bound to cause corruption in public life ; but while he was a Free Trader he did not coasider that policy a panacea for all ills, as side by side with the enormous growth in the wealth of the country they had a great increase in poverty. He therefore considered that Free Trade in itself had failed. With regard to the attitude of the Independent Labour Party to the other political partiest he might say the members of the organizatia were no opposed to the Liberal or Tory rank and file but they were opposed to the executives of both parties. Each consisted of capitalists and wa antagonistic to Labour. Mr. Seddon went on to denounce the expenditure of the Govern- ment during the past ton years, contending that it was wasteful as was shown by the fact that Sir John Fisher had just condemned such an enormous number of ships which had been built recently. That was proof that much of the money spent on the Admiralty was wasted, and the same applied to the army. There were, con- tinued Mr. Seddon, such an enormous number of persons either on or below the poverty line as to afford proof that the conditions under which society was carried on were a failure, and there ought to be a revo- lution in that respect. Every man had a right to live, and if he could work he had a right to employment. No man ought to be out of work simply because some other person could not make a profit out of him. Mr. Seddon went on to deal with the landpestion pointing out that if all the land were taxed at its present assessable value it would produce a revenue of £40.000,000. That would be sufficient to pro- vide old age pensions, and a free breakfast table, to defray election expenses, and also to pay members of Parliament, and there would still be money to spare to clear away insanitary arelre and slums, and to provide I meals for underfed school children. (Applause.) The usual votes of thanks concluded the meeting.

Meeting at Bank Quay. Mr. Seddon addressed a meeting in the Workingmen's Mission School„ Bank Quay, on Thursday evening. There was a fair attendance. Councillor Wright pre- sided and was supported by Councillor Stott, Mr. J. Podmore, junr., and Mr. A. Grimshaw. The CHAIRMAN replied to last weak's criticism in the " Examiner " of his policy in regard to the raising of Corporatioa officials' salaries. His contention was, he stated, that under-paid workers in humbler positions were entitled to be considered equally with the highly- paid officials. Mr. SEDDON devoted his address to a condemnation of the introduction of Chinese labour in South Africa, and explanation of the effects of the Sugar Convention. It was the Convention, he contended, which was mainly responsible for the rise in the price of sugar. Several questions from the audience were answered, and Mr. SEDDO N said that in his view the whole of the trouble in South Africa was caused by the cosmopolitan financiers whose sole interest was money-making. A vote of thanks was accorded to :gr. Seddon on the motion of Councillor STOTT.

SOUSA AND HIS BAND.

On Saturday March 4th, the renowned March King, John Philip Sousa, and his band will, through the ageney of Messrs R. H. Pearson Ltd., pay a return visit to Warrington when,—judging from the nature of his last reception—it requiries no great prophetic power to predict for him a warm weloome from crowded halls. Two performances will be given, at 3 in the afternoon, and 8 in the evening, under the patronage of the Mayor and Mayoress. Sousa has quite a new and extended repertoire. A new march "The Diplomat" and a new suite "At the King's Court "are promised us. Then in addition to some of those wonderful transcriptions for the military band of works by such classical composers as Liszt, Nicolai Mascagni, Eiger &c., there will be a reserve of new and popular " Sousa Encores." The Sousa orchestra will be supplemented by "Miss 111aud Powell, a clever violinist, and Miss Estelle Liebling, an equally popular soprano soloist. Two of Sousa's bandsmea will also take on solo work for cornet and saxaphone respectively.

A ST. HELENS MYSTERY.

Electrician Tries to Strangle His Wife

and Is Found Dela. A strange story was unfolded at St. Helens on Thurs- day night at an inquest on the body of Henry Heather (57), of Vincent-street, an electrical engineer and amateur photographer, who was found dead in bed. Mrs. Heather said her husband had been out of regular employment for about two years, and had been very despondent. "About three o'clock on Wednesday afternoon," she said, "he made two attempts to strangle ma and I left the house. He was very strange in his manner." "How do you account for it?" asked the Coroner.— He fancied, or seemed to think, that there was one too many in the house, add that, if he had to go, he would not go alone. I must go, too, and he would take rue. Mrs. Heather then stated that on leaving the house she went to a married daughter's, where she remained until someone told her that something had happened at home. She went back, and found her hasburl dead in bed, fully dressed. A bottle, which had contained cyanide of potassium, was found on thesink downstairs. Her husband had several bottles with chemicals in which he used in connection with photography. Dr. Knowles said that de ith was due to heart failure. There were no positive sympto its that the mat had taken poison of any kind. A post-mortem with chemical analysis of the contents of the stomach would be the only way of discovering whether he had actually taken poison or not. The jury returned a verdict that death was duo to heart failure, but there was no evidence to show how it was set up.

While hunting with the Wynustay Hounds, Colonel Dunn, of Althrey Hall, Bangor-on-Dee, met with a serious accident on Thursday. The meet was at Penley, and when nearing Peuley Dingle, the colonel's horse got his foot In a rabbit hole and fell heavily, throwing its rider and fracturing both bones of hi % legs below the knees.

COUNTY LICENSING SESSIONS.

Runcorn Bench and the "Long Pull."

At the annual licensing sessions at Runcorn on Mon- day Superintendent Eamon stated that there were 44 licensed viotuallers, 53 beersellers, and 4 others who sell off the premises—total, IM, giving an average of 208 of the population to each licence. One hundred and sixty- five persons had been proceeded against for drunkenness during the year, 148 of whom were convicted, as com- pared with 141 persons proceeded against and 135 con- victed during the preceding year. Of the persons pro- ceed against, 85 were residents and eighty non-residents. Fifty-seven of the licensees now gave what is commonly called the "long pull," and he asked the Bench to express disapproval of this pernicious practice. The Benchadjourned the case of the Millstone Inn until the adjourned sessions. The Chairman said the magistrates could not. speak too strongly concerning the "long pull," and they expressed the greatest disapproval of the practice. They would ask the police to go further into this matter during the next twelve months. They hoped a word to the wise would be sufacient..

Permission was gsven to alterations at the Cleveland Hotel, Greenway-road.

ONE "LONG-PULLER" IN THE DARESBURY DISTRICT.

Little public interest was taken in the annual Licens- ing Sessions held at Daresbury, on Tuesday. The magistrates present were—Mr. IL Neild (chairman), Mr. John White and Colonel Ridgway. Superintendent Ennion submitted his annual report with reference to the conduct of licensed victuallers and beer retailers in the Daresbury Division during the past year, from which it appeared that there are 23 licenced victuallers, one beer-seller, and two others who sell off the premises, giving an average of 363 of the population to each licence. He said that no licensed victualler had been proceeded against daring the year, and none had been proceeded against in the preceding year. No beer-seller had been proceeded against. Fifteen persons had been proceeded against for drunken- ness during the year, 13 of whom were convicted, as compared with 21 persons proceeded againstand 21 con- victed during the preceding year. The Superintendent reported that there was only one licence° in the division who gave what was commonly known as the "long pull." There were no objections to the renewal of licences.

The Chairman: This is a very satisfactory report compared with last year. As regards the "long pull " it ought to be given up as in Warrington,Liverpool, and other places. Perhaps the matter will be attended to. Superintendent Ennion promised that something should be done. .

The Clerk announced that all the licences would be renewed and the business of the Sessions was Concluded.

EDUCATION IN CHESHIRE.

Temperance Teaching in Schools. The Religious Instruction at Lynn Grammar

School.

At a meeting of the Cheshire Couaty Education Com- mittee, at Crewe on nonday, Dr. Hodgson presiding, two letters were read from the Bard of Education on the subject of the syllabus for temperance lectures in elementary schools. The Board pointed out that such instruction should be oa strictly scientific lines. lafore- over having in view the fact that instruction of this kind has two sides—the scientific and the moral or re- ligious—it is important that the local authority should be careful to see that a syllabus which was to be used for voluntary schools did not trench on those subjects of instruction which were especially reserved for the control of the managers of such schools. The Board expressed themselves as strongly of opinion on educa- tional grounds that, as far as possible, such lectures should be given by a teacher of the school. The Board hoped that the Cheshire Committee might be able to submit to them very shortly a revised syllabus which it would be possible to approve, as until this was done the Board could not consent to such lectures being given during the school hours. Dr. Hewitt proposed a resolution expressing regret that the 13aard of Education, while disapproving of the Cheshire syllabus of temperance science teaching, had not been able to specify any point in the syllabus to which they took objection. The committee, while asserting its right, under section 5 of the Education Act of 1905, to the control of all secular instruction given in the public elementary scheols for whose maintenance and efficiency the local educational authority is responsibile, is exceedingly desirous, as far as possible, to fulfil its obligations by harmonious co-operation with the Board of Education. Bat having considered the Foster memorandum, and the Board's comments thereon, the committee are still of opinion that, these lectures (which had been given in Cheshire for many years with the approval of previous Boards of Education, and with the general commenda- tion of the head teachers, many of such teachers having repeatedly testified to their knowledge of the scientific accuracy of the facts, and given in language well within the comprehension of the smallest child) should be con, tinned. In taking this course the committee are solely actuated by the ardent desire "to enable the children, not merely to reach their full development as individuals but also to become upright snd useful members of the community in which they live, and worthy sons and daughters of the country to which they belong." The Rev. T. J. Evans seconded the motion.

Judge Bradbury said that the committee must not take up such an attitude with the Board of Education as to risk the loss of the grant. The resolution was passed, and a deputation, consist- ing of Mr. C. E. Thorneycroft,Dr. Hodgson, Dr. Hewitt, and Professor Donner, was appointed to wait on the Board.

_ The Diroctor of Education reported that the govern- ing body of 111acclesfield High School for girls had passed a resolution that the school, together with its effects, be offered to the Cheshire County Council forth- with.

The Higher Education Committee recommended that they viewed favourably the taking over of the existing school for girlsat Nfacclesfleld ; but before committing the ratepayers to the additional expenditure required, the committee desired to investigate the approximate cost involved, and also to obtain the sanction of the Local Government Board for power to levy any extra rate beyond 2d in the pound that m 4 be required. The recommendation was adopted. The committee decided to call the attention of the Governors of the Lymm Grammar School to the fact that the present scheme under which the school is administered provides that the head master shall be a member of the Church of England. and that religions instruction shall he given in accordance with the doctrines of the Church of England, and that the Governors be informed that no further substantial aid will be given towards the maintenance of the school until arrangements are effected for the scheme to be amended in regard to these points.

THE POLICE OUTRAGE ON A CYCLIST.

The more the case of the cyclist,•G. Read, who was so outrageously treated by the Leicester police, is investigated the more it appears that the police acted not only in a high-handed manner but also quite illegally. A certified copy of the " order of dismissal " has been obtained by.the National Cyclists' Union from the Clerk to the magistrates at Leicester, and the following are extracts from that interesting document "And that the Court being of opinion that though the said charge is proved, the offence is of so trifling a nature that it is inexpedient to inflict any punish. ment cloth hereby dismiss the said information : But doth order that the defendant pay to the informant twelve shillings for costs forthwith. And in default of payment it is ordered that the said sums due be levied'by distress and sale of the defendant's goods, and in default of sufficient dis- tress that the defendant be imprisoned in His Majesty's prison at Leicester aforesaid for the space of five days." It will be noted that the,magistmtes were of opinion that no punishment should be inflicted, the offence being of so trifling a nature. They also very clearly directed that the costs should be collected by distress and in default of sufficient distress that the defendant should be imprisoned for five days. Although not a householder it was well known to the police that Read was in regular employment in Market Harboro, that ho was the owner of a bicycle, and they must have known that there would be no difficulty in securing payment of the paltry balance due. Those additional facts have been duly communicated to the Home Secretary but although a fortnight has elapsed since the first letter was sent, no reply, beyond a formal acknowledgement of the receipt of the letters, has been received by the National Cyclists' Union.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
PHYLLIS THE ELUSIVE.

'By Godfrey Dean.]

The Palace and the Priestess.

The knowledge of what Phyllis is to me somewhat alarms toy mascalineness at. times. Her refined per- sonality has me entirely in thrall. Love may become a complete obsession. • I dare not betray my feelings altogether—it would affright her to realise fully her power—so I mask them as much as I humanly can when in her company. Phyllis gave me a tender smile of welcome and beckoned me to a chair, in whose depths were many cushions.

Occasionally, when we are quite together, she gives me a glimpse into her soul, which makes my brain reel.

It would not be wise (if proper, according to con- vention) to let others see even a love-glance from her —out of respect for their peace of mind. Phyllis could inspire a rebellion. "I see," she said, "some writer, in an intimate article on the author of the book you hroaght me, has described him as a man of sentiment and imagination. What does he mean by that—by sentiment? "his author isn't sentimental.

I like Phyllis to come to me in this way, though not seldom she leads me, unsuspectingly, into difficulties. "It is the quality of fine feeling," I said. "That is what he is exactly—a man of sentiment Phyllis lost herself in reflection. Then she said, absent-mindedly, and in a whisper, "Yes, he could make love delightfully." Phyllis often says things in a way which leaves one deliciously in doubt as to her meaning. Did she mean--! But Phyllis usually holds herself in such delicate reserve that I dare not assume anything; even I, who—of course, she knows I love her. Her expression told me nothing. Really, Phyllis is most provoking. I arose and stood by the window, and thus chanced to see by a mirror that she was looking my way. " Women," I said, "can be guilty of refined cruelty." " Why? " she asked—all wonder. "A man in love," I explained, "has fine sensibilities."

"I thought you were speaking of women," she said.

Phyllis ought to be a diplomat, for she admits nothing, and thus compels me to explain everything myself. It was not the -,ort of thing to put into bara words; indeed, thus, it would appear ridiculous. I avoided a direct answer. "And if the woman does not aopreciate this he wil for ever disturb himself with doubts." "I should think," Phyllis said, with an impartial air, " that the woman naturally has more delicate sensibilities, and that the man should know it."

"There should be a perfect understanding between them," I said. "Some women "—Phyllis' head was bent—" don't like everything to_be taken for granted."

" A.re you one ?" " Whose tragedy is this ?" Phyllis asked, without loo' ing up. " Mine,' I said, in appropriate tone. With great deliberation she pretended to consider the matter, while I stood as at the bar of judgment. I attempted to sway her decision. "Phyllis, if you were to —that is, if anything were to happen—only one thing would remain for me." " What is that ?" "The life of a hermit. I would banish Myself from the sight of human beings—particularly women —and from civilisation."

. "It would be very cold and comfottless, living in a cave." I did not seem to have impressed her. live in a palace." "You wouldn't be a hermit, then; and not a bit interesting, not interesting enough for your friends to visit you. But how could you live_in a palace, and whose?" "My own. One I have myself erected, and have lived in, since I have known you." Phyllis moved her chair near the one I had left— why did I leave it? "What sort of a palace is it?" she asked. Phyllis was seated between me and my chair, and her face was turned in the direction of where I had sat. I prefer t ) see Phyllis's face—if she will permit it —when she speaks. That is why I took my chair again. "It's a wonderful palace," I whispered. 'What name do you give it?" "I call it the palace of Memory." "It can't be very substantial ? " " Well, such creations are not—ill a sense —but they arc sometimes very superior to others." Hay I come in then?" "You are always there." "And who else?" one but you." PAPER.

If one were asked, "What is the most übiquitous material in daily use in Great Britain? "the answer might well be," Paper." Few commodities weigh more in proportion to their bulk, and the bete noir of the railway porter is a trunk filled with books. It weighs like lead, and is responsible for more broken collar- bones than any other sort of luggage. In Far Cathay the making of paper from rice has been in vogue from time immemorial. Centuries before the Christian era, paper is supposed to have been fabricated in Egypt. Before the discovery of parchment, writing, however, was generally done on "papyrus," a species of Indian or Egyptian reed. Tha book of Josephus was thus inscribed on papyrus. It was amongst the treasures seized by Bonaparte in Italy and sent to Paris ; but it was restored in 1815. European paper was originally made of cotton. Rags were used for the same purpose in 1300. In 1866 wood was largely introduced into its manufacture, hop-stocks being used for the same purpose seven years later in France. But the world's out-put of paper is not confined to the pur- poses of writing or printing. In Japan, most of the houses are of oiled paper stretched across bamboo frames ; and in Europe pulped paper is converted into tables, chairs, carpets, barrels, boats, and so forth. In New Zealand it used to be possible to travel in a paper- made train—a material less liable to crack in the sun's heat than wood. Even pianos have been made with compressed paper cases. In Japan, pianos are made with keys of enamelled paper instead of ivory or 1 celluloid. For building purposes, paper has been com- pressed in America into bricks. In the Franco-Prussian War, thousands of French soldiers had the mortification of finding the soles of their boots had been made of paper instead of leather. The slime ssindle occurred in the Crimean War, and very possibly is being repeated f in the Russian War now.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 18, 1905.

to turn, with a om-irness mat was me cream. It smoothed out many wrinkles of unhappiness that had once been stamped upon his soul—yet still, it placed one other in the stead. He saw that here, in this hoOtropical climate—this place whose breath was like the breath of a panting wolf, and the atmosphere of which was as damp as the atmo- sphere of a Turkish bath—Cecil was growing old •almost before his time, that he was becoming infirm, and-losing the spirit that he once possessed in such exuberance. And the knowledge, as well as what he saw—as what he could not help seeing-- wrung his heart as in other and in happier sur- roundings, the increasing feebleness of a beloved ailing wife or child would have wrung it. And he would moan, and say to himself: "What is to become of me if he goes, and I am left here alone? Alone without a friend, a creature to care for me I What?" Yet he knew it had to be, that it must be soon. For they had been here seven years now, and Cecil presented all the appearance of a dog that was twelve years old. All its tan marks had long since grown white, its eyes were blurred and misty, it never jumped about kim, and barked and ran ahead of him, and leapt joyously when they went out in the cool of the dawn or of the sunset. But, instead, when it went with him—for it never refused to do that !—it walked slowly at his heels, limping sometimes, as though in pain, and throw. lag itself down upon the earth as though weary and exhausted whenever he stopped for a moment. And then—then—he would pick it up and carry it some distance, and, next, let it walk a little, and then carry it again. For Philip Horton loved the creature more and more as it gave signs of going away and leaving him alone in his desolate, mournful life; he loved it so much that, now, he would not have exchanged it and its companionship for his own life, and his own wealth, and the bride who was to have been his—before he became poor! He would never have done that—never! They had been too much to each other all these years for him to part with it. But at the end7of these seven years Horton fell' ill himself—ill of a low malarial fever, and with no one to look after him but an old Mestizo woman, and with no doctor but a local greaser, who came to see him and give him saline draughts. He became so weak and feeble that he could not move off his bed, and, at last, he believed that he was going to die. And he only had one thought that grieved him, only one pang that accompanied that belief. What would become of Cecil; who would there be to look after him as he grew more and more infirm—and—and—how would the creature bear his loss? What would become of it, what would its anguish be when it was taken away and, perhaps, flung into the sea as a useless, worn- out thing? God! what would pass through its mind as it was treated thus, because he had gone away and left it I _

"We have been companions so long," he said, stroking its head as it lay by his side—which it would scarcely ever lea-re—"we might have kept company together a little longer. Ali f Cecil, bitter as it would have been, you should have gone first, not I. I, at least, should have understood." Then, one day, as still he grew weaker and weaker, and the doctor's face grew longer and longer every time he came, Philip Horton raised himself on the low camp-bed on which he lay, and said, speaking firmly "Come, tell me the truth? Am I going to die?" For a moment the man did not answer, though he was used enough to telling people in that sickly, malarial place to prepare for the worst; but on Horton's insistence, he said, quietly: "Yes, I fear you are. You grow weaker, and your pulse gets higher: I fear it is so. Be brave! "Brave! Bali! What do you think I care? Except for one thing—this," and he touehed the head of the dog lying by his side. "Oh!" said the doctor, callously—what were dogs to the dwellers in Fort lilirabella but miserable, wretched creatures, that lived on the offal and scourings of the pestilential coast! "oh! you need not trouble. They say they are spreading contagion; they eat filth and then poison the atmosphere and the people they caress. They are all going to be killed. There is an order." "What! An order to kill them 1" "Yes. To take them all away and drown them. It will do good." "Will it? Well, we shall see."

After the man he:el gone, he lay thinking of this all day and on into the night; thinking of it, wondering if they would come and take Cecil before he died himself, or whether the moment safter he was dead they would take the poor, infirm thing and drown it. And, again, he wondered what the dog's feeling's would be at being dragged away from him who had protected and cared for it so. What

"Give me the mtdicine-ehest," he said to the native woman who waited on him that night. "Give it me—put it on the table by the bed. I may Want it." _ _

Then, when she had done so and gone away, he managed to open it, feeble as he Was and growing more so, and to regard its contents. They were imple enough, consisting only of those things that one takes to the tropics, and, as a rule, scarcely uses. But amongst them was a phial of laudanum, and from this he managed to draw the cork with his teeth.

But even as he did so, he knew that he was getting more feeble, as well as less clear-headed. The end, he felt, was very near. That cold, clammy perspiration, combined with a raging tem- perature, that shivering—that-- Where was he? And where was Cecil? Cecil—the only thing in all the world that loved him, that he had to love. Ah, yes. The dog was here, in its accustomed place, between his left arm and his side. It was always here now—always! It, at least, was faithful; a poor master was the same to it as a prince, or a millionaire, and it licked his hand, it was glad to be close to hint. To be with him. Delirious as he was now becoming, he knew that. "Had be done it," he wondered, a few minutes later. "Had he? Yts, he remembered. A second two he had opened the dog's mouth with his own and, and had poured the laudanum into it with the other. And it was so weak that it had not struggled; nay; it had licked his hand again—just before it gave one shudder, one convulsive grasp— and had then stretched itself out inside his arm, quite straight and with no movement ,except the quivering of its legs. And now it lay still in its own place—so still. How happy they were together! How happy now What did it matter that the light had gone out— that—that—he was 'going too ? They were together now. And they would be always.

AN' EXILE'S I)0G.

BY JOHN BLOTJNDELLE-BURTON Author of "Atross the Salt Seas," "Fortune's My Foe," "The Clash of Arms," "The Scourge of God," &c.

A man stood on the steps of a well-known hoiel in Southampton on a warm rainy night in November, and . looked up and down the 'deserted street, while seeing that there was scarcely a living creature in it. The rain persistent—and, if anyone was caught in it, drenching to the skin— had even driven away at last an organ-man, who had been grinding out his tunes, while the vendor of hot potatoes had departed, too. There was not a soul to be seen.

"Well," this n-..n said, as be knocked/the ashes out of his pipe and prepared to seek the warmer and more cheerful precincts of the hotel smoking- room, "it's suitable to me at any rate. My existence in England has come to an end in gloom, I may as well make my exit from the country in gloomy surroundings." Then he turned as though to follow out his intentions with regard to the smoking-room, yet paused in this action as if to take a final glance at the one miserable living creature which was in the street, and which he had for some time previous been regarding. This creature was a little, muddy, and rain- snaked fox-terrier pup which, ever since Philip Horton had been standing smoking on the hotel doorstep, had been whining piteously at the door of an empty house • across the road, and had also been shivering within its skin as only young pups of six or eight weeks old seem capable of doing. Nor had its whines appeared to have any effect, so far as compassion was concerned, upon anyone in the street, since only once had a window in the house next to the empty one been opened, and that only, to enable an elderly woman to fling a piece of coal at the miserable object below, and next, since this missile missed its mark, to empty a basin of dirty water on it. "Left behind," muttered Horton to himself as he now regarded the poor little beast for a second time, "left behind and forgotten. Or lost, and does not know where to go to. Poor little brute. It wants a friend badly. Well, so do I, Heaven knows. And if I were not going to South America to-night I'd be its friend. All the same I'll have a look at it.", Whereupon, suiting the action to the word, he went across the road, oblivious of the rain, and, approaching the pup, stooped down towards it, noticing how, at first, it shrank slowly away from him, then wagged feebly 'its little docked tail as it crouched over on to its side, and, next, licked his hand as he touched it gently. "Not badly bred," Horton said to himself, as now he lifted the little niite up and looked at it by the light of the street lamp; "and well marked. Yet almost starred, and wet and miserable. Poor little brute I" be said again, "poor little brute r" and he lowered his head and touched the dog's forehead with his cheek, and rubbed some of the wet off its back with the sleeve of a long frieze overcoat he wore. While as he did so the creature whimpered with pleasure, and put both its little fore-paws around his arm as though to hold Horton to it, and began to lick his hands furiously. "No home, no friends—nothing but the streets," Philip Horton said, "and no food. Nothing before you but starvation or the' mange and distemper, and then the docks with a stone round your neck. Why, even South America, with me for a master, would be better than that, wouldn't it, you miser- able little outcast, eh 7"

And for reply the little thing still licked his hands and clung to him tightly with those poor puny little 'fore-paws, and looked up at him with pathetic glances that might have stirred a heart .of marble. Which Philip Horton's was not. "Well." the latter said at last, half to himself and half to the dog, "I'll try. They shan't fling you overboard, I'll swear, once I have got you in the ship, no matter what their rules about dogs are; and if they want a fiver or a tenner for your passage, why! I'll pay it, poor as I am." After which he opened the front of his great frieze coat , and thrust the little thing inside, all muddy and wet as it was, and went back to the hotel. "I want a friend," he said to himself, "Heaven knows I ; and your kind, at least, are 'faithful, no matter whether we are poor or rich, or wild or steady."

"Fort Mirabella V.-Consul (vacant). Two hundred) and fifty pounds and fees," was what the reference books said at the beginning of the year, and it was the vacant vice-consulship which Philip Barton went forth to fill on that wet night. He went forth with bitterness in his heart if with no curses on his lips. He went into exile on two hundred and fifty pounds a year and fees, which, in good times, amounted to ten pounds annually— he who had started life with four thousand pounds a year, a house in Green-street, Hyde Park, and the prettiest promised bride that any man ever had. Now he had none of those things, and you: may imagine for yourself how he had become die- possessed of them. You can do it easily if you are a student of the society papers, as well as of those interesting records of the bankruptcy-court with which we are sometimes favoured, and if you know anything of the movements of worldly young women's In B,,its. Perhaps you have heard—on the stage if not in real life, as well as in -novels—of girls who have said that: "It is father, not I. You know that I should never go back on you," and so forth. And if you don't know you can guess, which will do just as well. Instead of all those favours of Fortune, Philip, had now got that vice-consulship in South America, ' which had been procured for him by a gouty and irascible uncle, who fervently hoped that his graceless nephew would never come back from it, though he was too much of a gentleman. to utter these thoughts to anyone. And he had also got a wooden house outside the town called Fort Mirabella, in which he slept and smoked, and drank a good deal of "Devil tickler," which was supposed to be whisky; and he possessed one or two acquaintances who had been farmers in England and were now engaged in the South American preserved meat trade, and who lived about twenty miles inland from the fort and came to see him about once a month. And these, with some half-castes and "greasers," were about all the people he had to exchange a word with. But he had got the dog I And he had christeneg it Samuel Hodgson, because that was the name of his late fiancee's father, whom he hated. Yet he had learnt to care for the creature so notch that he was thinking of calling it Cecil, which was the name of the girl he had once loved so madly, and —Heaven help him !—still loved. The ship's butcher looked after Samuel Hodgson —at that time unnamed; the skipper, who was a good fellow: although he did walk about what he called his "quarter-deck" in white kid gloves, made no bother over its being on board or sleeping in Horton's cabin, and so it arrived with him at Fort Mirabella and was, indeed, his one and only friend. Friend! Nay, it was more than that, far more. It was his alter ego, his other self. Him- self! They ate together of the same things, they lay together on the same bed, went out in the boat together. They were never apart. Let those who have led a lonely, solitary life, and especially those who have never seen any glimmering hope of the future being ever any better, but who have still had such a friend and comrade ag Cecil Hodgson—as the dog was now called—reflect upon what the creature had become to him aid he to it. and they will understand Philip Horton's feelings. !et as time went on, it brought easier reflections to Philip, even while, in another way, it threw its (lust of oblivion on much that had once 1... r...

WIDNES GAS ENGINEER'S SALARY.—EID INCREASE. At Widnes Town Council meeting on Tuesday the question of increases to salaries to various officials was brought up.—Alderman Timmis moved the minutes of the Gas and Water Committee, which contained the proposed increase in the gas engineer'e salary from £750 to £9OO. He did not wish to say much because the matter had been previously threshed out ; although he was aware there was a strong difference of opinion. I Mr. Carr asked to share some of the benefits which the town was enjoying through his management. The increase would bear comparison with • other neighbouring towns.—Mr. Hurst asked if the gas works belonged to the Corporation. Did the engineer hold any shares? And if he did not receive his increase, would he send in his resignation ?—To all these ques- tions Alderman Timmis replied that the works belonged to the'Corporation, the engineer could hold no shares and the latter question be could not answer, because he had not heard any such statement.—Many of the mem- bers were sore on the point of the increase, and as many were in favour of the showing of their apprecia- tion of the services of the engineer ; and in conse- quence on the vote being taken, eleven voted for and eleven against, so it devolved upon the Mayor to give his casting vote, which he did in favour of the increase. —Various increases to other officials of the Corporation were unanimous.

WARRINGTON CYCLING CLUB. !

A Successful Season Reviewed,

ANNIIAL MEETINC.

There is always a certain amount of pleasure and gratification in recalling the experiences of a successful cycling season and on Thursday evening a good muster of members of the Warrington Cycling Club listened with manifest interest to a review of the long series of outings and social functions which marked the year 1904. The occasion was the annual meeting which was held at the Patten Arms Hotel, under the presidency of the genial captain (Mr. D. Hodge). THE REPORT.

In presenting the 19th annual report, the hon. secretary (Mr. E. Pickavance) said it gave him great pleasure to inform the members that they had been favoured with a most successful season. 'I he weather, with the exception bf a cold damp Easter, had been all that could be desired. The sun had scarcely ever hidden his brightness from early spring to late autumn, and their runs together had been many, long and unchecked, through scenes continually glori ilied by the light of his smile. They could recall a delightful series of pleasnt runs among fair, unexpected nooks of nature, of exhjlarating spins over the breezy uplands midst sombre'pines lit up by golden bracken, of sweet indolent loiterings through shady avenues and along the banks of reedy meres and rippling streams ; strolls through forest glades, mid banks of glowing heather and dreamy sails up the lovely winding reaches of the river Dee—an unbroken series of pleasurable wanderings, which could well compare, if not excel, the experiences of any other season.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RUNS DESCRIBED.

The opening run took place on Saturday, March 26th, Warburton being again selected for the fixture. The weather was dull and cold, but about 20 members and friends assembled at Mrs. Barff's hospitable board. A game of bowls before tea, and a convivial evening after- - wards brought to a close a most enjoyable opening fix- ture. On Saturday, April 30th, their usual inter-club run with the Manchester Wheelers took place, Hoo Green being the venue. Over 20 wheelers met nearly the same number of "our boys" and indulged in a friendly bout at bowls. The War- ringtonians's superior knowledge of the " geography " of the green again told and they retired victors by 23 points. The opening Tuesday night run was held on May 2nd. The night was cold and showery, but over 30 members and friends arrived in time for the smoker at Sankey. The captain (Mr. D. Hodge) occupied the chair, songs of various natures were rendered by numerous artistes, and a most enjoy- able evening was the result. On Saturday June 25th Ladies Day was the fixture, Lower Peover being the destination. Unfortunately the clerk of the weather was in a bad humour, and owing to the showery nature of the weather only three ladies turned out, but despite all drawbacks a pleasant afternoon was spent. On Saturday July 9th the members were again entertained by their worthy President (Mr. Naylor) and his good lady at Beeston Towers. The weather ,was glorious, and over forty members accompanied by about 30 lady friends were present, in aadition to a numerous house party of Mr. Naylor's. The usual high class "alfresco " tea was done justice to, and then, proceeding to the lawn in front of the house,a couple of hours were accorded to song and jest and merry dance. After hearty thanks to the host and hostess, the National Anthem, and ringing cheers, the party started for home. The return journey was a treat, the glorious ride across Delamere Forest being superb. " ThE meadow fields were waving in the sunset like a sea; a billowy deep whose flowers flashed like the foam." This fixture must undoubtedly be classed as one of the red letter days in the annals of the club, and their sincerest thanks were due to their hospitable president for his kind thoughtfulness for their interests. On Saturday, July 23rd, another inter-club run with the Manchester Wheelers took place, the destina- tion being Cranage. After tea a cricket match was pro- ceeded with. The wheelers bated first and totted up a total of 63, but when the Warrington side went in the shades of night were drawing in. However after amassing 22, for 2 wickets. the Jessop of the Warrington side hit the ball into a field of corn, and the match had to be declared a draw. On Saturday August 6th, they again honoured the fixture known as president's day.A splendid turn out of 37 members and friends arrived at Chester and proceeding to the river, sailed by the club's private cruiser (belonging to Mr. Capner), to Eccleston Ferry. On Saturday, September 17th, the annual Fishing Matah was held at Acton Bridge. The weather was ideal and nearly 30 members arrived at the river's brim. The con- test was entered into with great spirit, one enthusiastic angler trying to annex the finny tribe with a bun for bait. After the match, the potato weighing machine was brought out and after careful manipulation it was found that J. Podmore had taken the Ist prize, J. H. Smith 2nd and F. Garnett the 3rd. The final Tuesday night run took place on September 23nd. A hot pot supper was first partaken of by between 20 and 30 mem- bers, and afterwards an impromptu smoker was held and a jolly evening brought to a close a successful series of night runs. On Saturday October Ist, the closing run of 1904 took place, Knutsford being the venue.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
SPORT, &C;

The ancient game of bowls had proved most attrac- tive on Wednesday evenings. The Chapel House green had been well attended every week, and the members were to be congratulated on haying secured this green for one night of the week for their exclusive use. Two mains had been played, the prizewinners for the first being Messrs. F. Redfern, G. Bowman and J. Bellian, and for the second Messrs. F. Barber, G. H. Smith, W. Unsworth and E. Pickavance. A bowling match was also played with the Widnes C.C., which resulted in a victory for the visitors by 11 points. Another feature of the season was a cricket match played on the War- rington ground between the cycling club and a Warrington eleven. After a good match they were just defeated by one run.

THE HOLIDAY TOURS.

The holiday tot7M--(triais-iiin-e—li.; might say, per i cycle in many nstances) had been greatly enjoyed. SOCIAL FUNCTIONS

The social side Oiti;--pita-t: season had without doubt proved an unqualified success. The Billiard Handicap, now in its closing stages, attracted an entry of 38 mem- bers. and the best thanks of the Club were due to Mr. W. E. Kiernan, of London, for so kindly presenting them with the prizes for it. The match resulted in a win for the Wheelers, the Warrington team only managing to win two games out of the eight played. Afterwards the combined teams took ten together. Three first-class smokers had been given during the past season. and, needless to say, had given great pleasure to all who had the privilege of being present. The best thanks of the club were due to the many artistes, who so willingly gave their services at these functions, which did so much to keep up the popularity of the club and served to while away many a winter evening.

THE POSITION OF TILE CLUB.

The club numbers now stood at 82 active members and 11 honorary members which was an increase of 9 over last year and which proved at once, that the club was in no immediate dangler of dying out from lack of members. The treasurer's balance sheet showed that after meeting all expenditure their balance bad still further increased. That was extremely gratifying and proved that they had a good man in the parson of their Chancellor of the Exchequer. There had been eleven Committee meetings during the year. They bad been . well attended, and the business promptly and efficiently dealt with. He took that opportunity of thanking the committee personally, and the members generally, for the kindly support and sympathy which he had at all times received from them, during his many years of office,and especially he tendered his best thanks to Mr. F. Garnett for so kindly taking over the secretarial duties for a couple of months during his I recent great bereavement. g.'heir thanks were due to the local press for their excellent reports of the various functions of the club, also to Miss Kiernan for the free use of rooms for the r any meetings and social gatherings they had held, also to Mr. W. Unsworth for his liberal terms with regard to the Chapel House Green, and to all who had helped to make the past season such a great success. _ _

ORITTJAR Y. It was with ieelings of the deepest sorrow that they had to record the loss during the year of two old and valued past supporters of the club, Mr. E. Pickavance an old Vice-president, and Mt. W. Bellian fan ld member, who for some years regularly attended their rune. The club sent beautiful floral tributes, and was officially represented at both funerals, and he could assure the members in all sincerit3, that both the bereaved families felt deeply grateful for their kind sympathy.
