Samuel Grindley. of Latchford Without, appeared at the Daresbury Sessions, on Tuesday, in answer to a summons charging him with persistent cruelty to his wife Elizabeth.—Mr. Browneappeared for the complain- ant and Mr. Steel defended.—lt was stated that efforts to dispose of the case without bringing it before the Court • had failed, the difference being as to the amount of alimony—Mr. Browne asked the Bench to order the defer lent to pay £1 a week towards the support of his wife and thwe children. Warrington v. Oldham.

This return match, which had an important bearing on the championship, aroused .a lot of interest at Warring- ton, and a crowd of about 6,000 people assembled to itness the struggle. Warrington were without tlnckenhall, otherwise both teams were at full strength. !rine weather favoured the fixture and the ground was In capital order., Mr. C. Gibson (Salford) was the referee and the teams lined out as follows : Warrington.—Hallam, back; Fish, Isherwood, Brooks, and Kenyon, three-quarter backs; Bate and Davies, half backs; Shugars, Thomas, Boardman, Cook, Belton, joiler, Swift. and Naylor, forwards. LANCASHIRE COMBINATION. WARRINGTON " A " v. OLDHAM "A." Played at Watersheddings, Oldham, before a large attendance. Warrington faced a strong breeze in the opening half, and following the usual exchanges, a spell of give-and-take play was witnessed at half-way. Old- ham pressed as the result of strong forward play, but a couple of, penalties were well utilized by knight, and the venue was changed. The Warrington backs passed. and both Dickenson and Harris(made bold attempts to cross. Oldham again got down to the Warrington end as the result of strong forward work, and after Knight had saved Carpenter almost placed a goal from a diffi- cult angle. Barker was next given a chance, but failed to escape Harris. Oldham continued to attack, only to meet with a resolute defence, and at the interval the score sheet was blank. Turning round, Warrington were soon pressing, and clever forward work led up to Berry scoring. This was the only tangible record, and War- rington thus won by three points to nil. *Swinton (A), 3 points; Widnes (A) 2 points. Wigan (A), 247 points ; Salford (A), 0.

CHESHIRE JUNIOR LEAGUE. WALKER'S BREWERY V. RUNCORN RESERVES. - This match, which practically decided the champion- ship, was played on the ground of the former on Satur- day. The visitors kicked off against a strong breeze, and the home team were immediately on the attack. At half time they had registered a(goal) scored by Timmins) to nil. Re-starting, the visitors worked their way into t)ae home team's quarters, and from a scrimmage near the line, Brown dashed over and scored. The goal kick was a failure. The home forwards now worked hard, and Jackson scored at the corner from a pass by Moosey. Timmins failed at the goal kick. The home team again were soon on the offensive, and from some good work done by Gallop, Allen scored at the corner, after run- ning nearly half the length of the field. Gallop missed the goal. Result: Walker's Brewery, eight points; Runcorn Reserves, three points.

FRIENDLY MATCHES. PADGATE R.F.C. V. RYLANDS.

- On the ground of the first named,this being Padgate's last match before disbanding. Padgate kicked off be- fore a strong wind, Warren returning. From a scrim- mage in the home half, a round of passing ensued be- tween Philips, Cook, Cottam and Furness, ended in the latter outpacing all opposition and scoring a splendid try behind the posts. Bate converting. Half-time, Padgate 5, Rylands,'o. After the interval the homesters pressed but nothing tangible resulted. Padgate screwed the pack and Ashton and Leslie had hard lines In not scoring. Stedman broke through for the visi- tors but was well brought down by Furness. Rylands's were penalised for offside end 'ft,:gg placed a fine goal, Ryland's forwards rustled tue scrimmage, but with a clear field failed to score and Moseycleared the danger. Result, Padgate 7 points, Rylands nil.

EARLESTO WN F 01716 RY FATALITY.-INSPECTION _ _ OF MACHINERY NECESSARY. _

Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest at Earlestown Town Hall, on Monday, respecting the death of Thomas Monaghan, aged forty-eight, labourer, of Liverpool-row, Earlestown, who died on Tuesday from the effects of a crane jib falling upon him at the Vulcan Foundry on the Ist inst. The evidence showed that the deceased and two others were removing a mo- tion-plate from a machine to a trolly, when the jib shaft came out from a top bracket. The crane had been erected by Michael James Ryan,ii skilled mechanic. Mr. Lewis, his Majesty's workshops inspector, said he had no hesitation in saying that the crane had not been properly erected. Every available eighth of an inch of the jib pin should have been taken advantage of. The crane should also have been regularly inspected. The jury in returning a verdict of " Accidental death," recommended periodical inspection. The Coroner re- marked that there ought to be a legal obligation put upon employers to inspect such things as cranes Mr. Lewis : It is coming. The Coroner : But very slowly. The House of Commons will think about it twenty years from now. •

Diagram 62 ehows a position for a run-through cannon, and the cannon is easy enough even fox a very ordinary player The chief thing in playing such a cannon ie to play it with sufficient strength to bring the white ball back from the baulk cushion to the other balls. If this is done good position is almost sure to be left. Most likely the balls will be placed for an easy cannon, or an in-off from the white into the centre pocket may be on. Or the red may be well placed for a pot or an in-off. Of course even if the rux-through cannon were played with such bad strength that the white ball remained in baulk or only came out a little way, the red might still be well placed for the centre pocket; on the other hand, if the cue ball were to catch the red badly and the white ball did not come up far enough, there would most likely be nothing at all easy for the next stroke.

Most ordinary players, when playing, run- throughe aim to hit the object ball with the same degree of fulness irrespective of whether they are playing the stroke at slow, medium, or fast pace. Allowance for pace must, however, always he made, for the stronger the stroke the more will the cue ball be deflected from the line of aim. For example, if the balls be placed in a position similar to the sue shown on

DIAGRAM a THE EFFECT OF STRENGTH IN RUM -HROUGH smoxvit

Diagram 63, a run-through cannon is quite a simple stroke, but if the stroke be played at a high pace, and the red be taken no fuller than when making the cannon at medium pace, the cue ball will be thrown off more or less as shown by the intersected line on the diagram. All players are aware of the great differenCe that exists between the throw-off Angle of a medium- pace half-ball stroke and a forcing half-ball stroke, but comparatively few ordinary players seem to be aware that pace has Ile same effect in run-through strokes, though strong run- through .strokes might very aptly ha called forcing run-through'. When playing strong run-through cannons, if note be taken of the strokes that fail, it will be found that the cannon is almost invariably missed through the cue ball passing the second cbject ball on the outside (as shown by the intersected line on diagram 621.; When playing strong run-throughs it will therefore be safer for ordinary players to always aim fuller on to the object ball than may seem necessary to them, and poor players might well do this in any kind of run-through stroke. When playing strong run-through cannons with com- position balls of any kind still fuller aim than is ih.cessary with ivory balls must be taken on to the first object ball for the simple reason that pace increases the off throw-off angle to an even greater extent with composition balls them it does with ivory balls. Even when play- ing medium-pace run-throughs with composi- tion balls aim should be taken just a shade fuller on to the object ball than is necessary with ivory balls.

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE. FIRST DIVISION.

EARLESTOWN Y.M.C.A. V. CROSFIELD'S.—At Earles- town on Saturday. The visitors had a weak team doing duty. Crosfield's started against a still breeze and attacked, the final shot going over the bar. Earles- town, aided by the wind, got near the visiting goal and being awarded a penalty scored a somewhat lucky goal. This aroused the visitors who several times were dangerous but shot wide. From a corner Warren missed an open goal by shooting over the bar. In the second half Crosfleld's asserted themselves, and Strettle had hard lines with a good "pot "shot which hit the inside posti and came out again. Strettle sewed from a penalty. The Y.M.C.A. went to the • other end and obtained a very soft goal. Llewellyn, one of the visitors, was hurt and retired from the play. The next item was a grand bit by Ritchie, who went " on his own "through the opposition finishing up by scoring a capital goal. Earlestown won rather luckily by 3 goals to 2.

SECOND DIVISION.

ST. MARY'S RESERVE V. STOCKTON HEATH RESERVE. —On the former's ground on Saturday. The "Heathens" were awarded a free kick in the first minute, but nothing resulted. Exciting play ensued and half-time arrived with the 'visitors leading by one goal (scored by Grainger) to nil. After the interval both sides scored once. Final: Stockton Heath, two goals; St. Mary's one goal.

THE FREE CHURCHES.

ANNUAL MEETING OF THE WARRINGTON COUNCIL.

The President and the Education Question.

THE REV. J. G. GREENOUGH ON UNITY

The Rev. J.. G. GREENOUGH, M.A., ex-president of the National Council, was the principal speaker at the annual meeting of the Warrington and District Council of Evangelical Free Churches which was held in Wycliffe Congregational Church on Wednesday evening. There was an encouraging attendance, Mr. C. E. Parker (president of the Council) being in the chair. Amongst others present in addition to the Rev. J. G. Greenough were: The Revs. John Yonge, G. H. Carnson, J. S. Hughes, H. Wall, C. L. Stowe, and F. Vaughan, the Rev. J. F. Matthews and Mr. R. Pierce Williams (hon. secretaries) and Mr. Robert Henshall, THE WORK OF THE YEAR.

DATE OF OPENING OF THE WINWICK HALL. The Rev. J. F. 3LiTTHEws presented an interesting statement on the work of the year, observing at the out- set that he was delighted with that representative gathering, which was certainly the largest Free Church gathering they had had for some years past. One matter which had been engaging the attention of the Council during the past twelve months particularly was the eetension and progress of the work connected with their Winwick Hall scheme. About three months ago the memorial stone was laid by Mrs. Parker and a very successful and hippy afternoon gathering inaugurated the new building. He was happy Ito tell them, on the authority of the builder whom he had seen that week, that the work had so far progressed that they would be able to have the building opened by the 14th March. (Applause). He was also delighted to announce that his good friend Dr. James Hope Moulton, of Didsbury College, had promised to preach the opening sermon. Dr. Moulton, it was inter- esting to note, was the president of the Free Church Council at Cambridge when a similar scheme was inaugurated and he would therefore be present at the completion and opening of a second hall. He had addressed a letter of congratulation to the Council upon taking that forward movement and ventured to express the opinion that it would be an exceedingly good thing for the country at large if in all parishes where Free Churches were not represented similar schemes could be taken up. Continuing Mr. Matthews said the Free Church Council had for some years been responsible for the holdirg of weekly services in the Workhouse. These services had not only ' grown in interest but they had during the year actually doubled in numbers. Where some twelve or fifteen months ago they had an attendance of thirty or forty they now had from eighty to ninety and sometimes a a hundred. (Applause). Miss Crampton kindly con- tinued to give her services, playing the organ and visiting the sick and infirm inmates of the institution. lln regard to the financial position, they had up to the end of December spent about £25 and the income had been about £l4 so that they owed to the treasurer £ll, to say nothing of the money they had spent from the beginning of this year to the present time. Although he was no beggar, he would venture to put in a plea to that representative gathering for assistance in carrying on the work. From what he had reported they would see that that was by no means a dead Council. They were trying to do all they could and they would do more if they had a much larger ex- pression of sympathy on the part of the churches. There had gone forth from the National Council a re- quest that in all big centres of population a YOUNG FREE CHURCHMEN'S LEAGUE

should be -started to study Free Church principles, and for the training of young men in Free Church history and in the art of public speaking. They had been very diffident in forming such a league in Warrington because there were so many meetings held already that one was afraid of multiplying such concerns, lest, like some others, they should go to the wall. If there was anything like the feeling of the churches represented that night that such a league would do good the Council would like to know it and to act on any suggestion which might be made. He would respectfully urge all the churches to see that their delegates attended the executive and Council. He hoped the inspiration they received from that meeting would result in increased numbers and interest and that it would eery soon effect a large movement of spiritual progress in the town they loved so much. (Applause). THE PRESIDENT AND THE EDUCATION QUESTION,.

The PRESIDENT, who- was cordially received, said he made no apology that evening for dealing with the question of national education. The subject was ever important, but it was specially so in view of the injus- tice which Free Churches recognized in the Education Act of 1902. (Hear, hear.) The State, for its own welfare as a social organization, had taken:over the education of its future citizens, deeming it to be a danger to the State that children should grow up untaught. In times past many of the departments of public life were discharged by the Church of England. The care of the poor, administration of wills, schooling, hospitals itc„ all the social duties in connection with these were at one time discharged by the Church. When great sections of the nation split of from the national Church, on doctrinal and other grounds, it wasl impos- sible for the Church to continue discharging these national obligations. Gradually these things had been taken over by the people and the State. In the trans- ference there had been a certain amount of friction, and in none more so than in the case of the schools. We were now in the transition state. The question of the control of the education of the children, especially in religious matters, was a very difficult one. Some found considerable difficulty in making up their minds about the Fiscal question, which was at present creating such a stir in the political and commercial world. To his mind the Fiscal question was AS clear, and as simple as a proposition in Euclid to a trained mathe- matician—(hear, hear)—and that because the basis of the subject lay altogether in material things But the education question lifted them to a far higher plais, and into regions of thought and ethics, which were to a great extent unexplored, orl at any rate unsurveyed. They had to determine the relative authority of the parent, the Church, and the State, and in attempting this they revealed some of the most fundamental and long standing difficulties which had divided men for centuries. To his mind, to harmonize the interests of parent, Church and State, (and when he spoke of Church he meant all denominations) in a national system of education was a practical impossi- bility, and he was doubtful whether any satisfactory compromise could be come to between the three interests , which did not involve a sacrifice of fundamental ideas. (Hear. hear.) The only direction in which he saw day- light was for

===== 0002601_19050218_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHURCH TO WITHDRAW

from the position of partner in this matter, and leave the question to be decided between the parents and the State. And here he thought lay the fundamental mistake in both the Education Acts of 1870 and 1902, although he thought it was possible that these were necessary steps in the process of transition. Both these Acts involved a partnership between parties whose interests were irreconcilable. The only logical solution and outcome of a State controlled education, was a State paid, education and the compromise of a jointly controlleo and jointly paid education would never work because it involved compromises in those things which were fundamental. The State must have its own school. provide and pay its own teachers. (Applause.) If the ground were cleared in this way, he did not think it would be difficult for the State, and the parents who formed the State, to agree as to those things in which a child should be educated. The people of this State believed that the world was round, or as they used to be taught, like an orange. There were a few who still held that the world was flat, but in a universal system must they not be governed by the general beliefs? In like manner the people of the State believed in the Christian truths taught in the Bible, a.s being THE BEST BASIS 01, MORAL CULTURE. Were they to exclude this because a small minority thought the teachings of the Bible erroneous? He was well aware that there were many of their friends, who held religious teaching in the highest esteem, contended that it was no business of the State to teach moral and religious truths, and who considered that in taking this position they were taking the only logical one. He failed to see anything logical about it. He might be wrong, he was only stating the case as it appeared to him. The State for its own safety had taken over education : to omit from its curriculum the highest form of education, that of the moral factiltieswhich was the greatest safeguard to the State, appeared to him to conflict with the reasons for which the State undertook the task. (Hear, hear.) For surely a State ran far more danger from citizens growing up void of moral and religious light, than it did from growing up void of intellectual culture. This country was a Christian country as well as ao intellectual one. But many of them might say in their minds that was all very well up to that point, but where was he going to draw the line between religious truths about which there was a general consensus of belief, and those truths which were of a sectional and denominational I:iaTacter. He repiled that he was not anxious to draw lines. The Price of Sugar.

Sir,—Certain statemeEts have appeared in the Liver- pool daily papers during the past week which have led to a good deal of misapprehension as to the present position of the sugar market and its future prospects. It may be interesting to know that the retail prices in Liverpool and district have ruled higher than those ,charged in Warrington and the neighbourhood, and that the so-called reduction so widely and sensationally announced does not denote that fall in the price of sugar that many cursory readers ef the public press so eagerly anticipated. As a matter of fact, the drop in the market price from the highest point is only Is. per cwt., and this does not affect all grades. it really represents about one-eighth of a penny per pound, a fraction of value which is unknown to our present oinage.—Yours &c.,

J. P. GORST, Secretary, Warrington Grocers' Association.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Lloyd-George's Speech. "Mr. A. H. Crostield Replies to Tory Criticism.

_ Mr. A. H. Crosfield has addressed the following letter to the observer" : Sir,—Your notes on Mr. Lloyd-George's magnificent speech follow a plan which may be effective in so far as you are catering for readers who neither heard it nor saw a report of it, and for that reason they deserve an answer. You have ignored the whole impressive argu- ment, as well-reasonal as it was eloquent, which formed the belk of his speach, in order to wrest a few statistics from their context. In one instance you have scored a point. When Mr. George spoke of our Exports to the United States as forty-one millions be obviously made a slip of the tongue. But that does not invalidate his argument. It is still a sterling fact that despite a pre- ference we send only twelve millions worth of goods to Canada, while all the "scientific tariffs" of the McKinleys and the Dingleys do not prevent us from selling to the value of over twenty-two millions to the United States. I turn to your main point. Mr. Lloyd George in his happiest and wittiest vein pointed out that despite Mr. Chamberlain's lugubrious prophecies of declining trade and dwindling exports, the Board of Trade returns actually show that our exports have increased by six- teen millions since Mr. Chamberlain began to prophesy • our ruin. This you say is to " misrepresent facts ' and you charge Mr. Lloyd George with ignoring "the fact that that increase is almost entirely accounted for by the increase in a single industry, the Cotton Trade,and • the additienal fact that the increase in cotton is largely accounted for by the increased coet of raw material and consequent inflation of the export figures." This is de- licious. Why must we ignore cotton? To be sure Mr. Chamberlain may well feel a grudge against it. It is one of those obstinate trades which so stupidly declines to square with his prophecies. But the rest of us cannot afford to waive it aside in this airy way. It may only be one trade, but it happens to provide a livlihood for many thousands of our people in Lancashire. Mr. Chamberlain has told us that "cotton is going." With all respect to you, Sir, and at the risk of being denounced as traitors and Pro- Boers for the heresy, we cannot help objecting that cotton is doing very well. Your second point is sheer a priori guess work. It sounds plausible aud almost intelligent, and yet it is hopelessly wrong. The increaue in cotton exports is not due to the increased cost of raw material. The whole Cotton Trade during 19V expanded, not merely in value, but also in bulk. To begin with the imports of raw cotton increased from 16,009,813 cwts in 1903, to 17,454,897 cwts in 1904—an increase, if you please, in bulk. The exports increased for yarn from over 150 million lbs to over 163 million lbs. In piece goods the increase was from 5157 million yards, to 5591 million yards. Can facts and figures speak more plainly.? Finally you state that cotton accounted almost entirely" for the total increase of the national experts. But why these adverbs ? It is just as easy to give the figures a.nd much more satisfactory to be precise. The increase due to cotton was 10 millions. The total in- crease was 16 millions. Is ten almost 16 ? So much for " Observer's " charge that Mr. Lloyd George " misrepresented the facts." Now let me point out that" Observer "has totally failed to follow the nimble Celtic wit of his adversary. He seems to have read the speech carefully and yet he failed to see that Mr. Lloyd George was chaffing Mr. Chamberlain all the while. Free Traders have never said that all is for the best in the best of all possible worlds when our exports show an increase. On the contrary, despite the expansion of our exports we are passing through a period of trade depression. We may be send- ing more cotton abroad partly because the home market is weak. It is Mr. Chamberlain who said that exports are the test of the nation's .prosperity. So that the answer is, "No. Thanks to your war and your reckless expenditure we are as a nation not doing as well as we could wish. And yet our exp,orts increase." What is the moral? Will " Observer in his Notes " next week point it for us? Presumably he does not regard Mr. Chamberlain as a erentice hand at economics, and so we shall await with. interest the conclusion which he thinks ought to be drawn. Exports are not in them- selves a test of prosperity, any more than large im- ports mean ruin. There is one country in Europe which satisfies Mr, Chamberlain's two tests. Year by year its exports increase; year by year its exports show an excess over its imports. Happpy Land !Fiscal Paradise! And yet that land is—Servia. The more of its rulers it murders, the further it plunges into debt, the lower its ,credit falls, and the more it must send abroad to pay its creditors, and the less it can afford to buy abroad. Arc we to follow the lead of Eervia ? A nice illustra- tion truly to this precious theory! With this little clue to guide him perhaps "Observer" will be able to read Mr. Lloyd George's speech over again—and under- stand it. One other point "Observer" makes against Mr. Lloyd George which needs a brief answer. Mr. Lloyd George stated that the unemployment in this country,bad though -it is, is not to be compared with that which prevails in America. That is notoriously true. "Observer" how- ever replies that our labour statistics show an average of 71 per cent, out of work among our own Trade Unionists, while the figure for American Unions is not much over 2 per cent. In point of fact the percentage of,unemployed was given on Thursday morning as 6'7 for January, and in fixing that percentage the Board of Trade went outside the Unions, taking 3,060 of its returns from employers and only 1,294 from Unions. As for America, Unionism has nothing like the extension there that it has here. It is strong only in a few dis- tricts, and there only in a few highly skilled trades. A percentage of from 2 to .3 among these affords no clue whatever to the mass of unemployment. The same thing is true of Germany. Finally " Observer " tries to frighten us with the terrible things which would happen to us if Canada should withdraw her preference in our favour. Has he forgotten that Mr. Chamberlain pointed out at the last -Colonial Conference that this Preference is amere illu- sion? No country can give the colonies more favour- able treatment than we do. We have given them a free _market for everything they produce and free Govern- ment for themselves, and we defend them into the bar- gain. And" Observer" is very much mistaken if he thinks they are unappreciative and unmindful of this open-handed Eot to say handsome treatment. Let us strengthen the ties of Empite by all means, but let us do so by rational raeans.—Yours, etc.,A. H. CROSFIELD.

Hoylake, Feb. 17th, 190.1.

(Applause). THE REV. J. G. GREENOUGH'S ADDRESS.

FREE CHURCHES MORE UNITED THAN THE ESTABLISEIED CHURCH. THE EDUCATION QUESTION.

The Rev. J. G. GRENNOUGH, who was heartily greeted said he had listened vvith very great pleasure to the chairman's lucid address, and he rejoiced to be at that meeting and to find that the Free Churches of War- rington were fairly Nvell represented. He was sorry to hear they were in debt,but of course that was inevitable simply because they were alive. (Laughter). A vigorous institution was always in debt. It was only the decayed institutions, the antiquated, endowed institutions that owed nothing, and every church and institu- tion which had real life was sure to be in debt. He was glad to see they were united, not only in Christian thought and aspirations, but also in Christian work. One of the best features of the Federation movement was that it had brought , the churches really together ha joint action and joint I warfare. They had had a sentimental union among the churches for a great many years, and endless talk about their oneness. He thought he had heard at a hundr d tea meetings at least, Nit.. Jones, the Baptist, say of Mr. Smith, the Wesleyan, that he loved him as a brother —(laughter)—and he had heard Mr. Smith reply with great unction that there was no one in the world, except his own wife, whom be held in such peculiar affection as :Mr. Jones. (Laughter.) Then it had all evaporated in steam, passing apprehensions, and senti- ment, and there had been an end to it. To•day, think God, they were

===== 0002601_19050218_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
'UNITED IN MISSION WORE

all over the country, united against the enemies of the Lord, the real foes of this land—social evils, intemper- ance, unbelief, and injustice of every kind. (Hear, hear). lie could remember the time when things were very different, when the sects were sects unmistakably, in the narrow, invidious sense of that term, when they were almost jealous of each other and could hardly spare a" G od-speed "to each other. Thare was a good Baptist in Yorkshire who was heard praying at a prayer meeting and he said: "0 Lord, Thou knowest we are' not getting on very well in our Church, but we thank Thee that the Wesleyans are not getting on any better and the Independentslare a great deal worse." (Laughter.) There was a great deal of that spirit about as was instanced by the high wall in the shape of Calvinism and Arminianisrn which formerly divided the Baptists and the Methodists. Now they were devoutly thankful they had buried all that and forgotten it. It was as far away as Noah's Ark. (Laughter.) They had gathered round the uplifted Christ, lifting Him as He ought always to have been lifted, above every creed, dogma, party, and sect, and round His blessed and uplifted Person they had realized the union for which He prayed. Some people told him—he heard it very often now from sanguine people—that by and by not long hence they would have swallowed up all the denomina- tions into

===== 0002601_19050218_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
ONE GREAT FREE CHURCH..

-- Well, he thought that was a long way off still, and he did not know he would like to bring it much nearer than it was. He thought the denominations could be perfectly loyal to each other, thoroughly brotherly and united, and yet not obliterate themselves. He remem- bered the great thinker and apostle said that through the Church should be made known the manifold, or many-coloured wisdom of God. That could not be done if the Church was to be all of one colour. They had thrown down the denominational walls—they could not look at each other over them—but there was no need to blot out the lines, because they could shake hands, and embrace, and kiss each other over the lines, and that was all they needed. They knew they were united already in all essentials—they were one great Free Church. They were far more one Church than was that Church which was always calling itself one and perhaps boasting of its oneness. They were far more united to-day than the Established Church was. (Hear, hear). Everyone knew that. Union there was more artificial and nominal than real and vital. They were united by certain very dear historical traditions ; and they were united by thirty-nine articles, most of which they did not believe—(laughter)—by the use of a common book of prayer which they interpreted very variously, and fl nally they were united by what he called a State chain, the weight of privilege and endowment which probably did more to Irgep them together than all the other links. (Laughter.) The very theory of a State Church was to promote and preserve union and to prevent diversity and what they called schism. The Established Church had had scores of Acts of Parliament passed for that one end—to make and keep it one and to prevent divergence and disunion. Yet, in spite of that the Established Church, by the confession of its own members, was

THE MOST DIVIDED CHURCH IN CHRISTENDOM. They of the Free Churches who had no Acts of Parlia- ment, no outside forces, no law to bring them together, without all those helps andsimply by the mighty Sp rit of God shedding abroad divine love in their hearts, had reached a lagre measure of blessed unity. (Applause.) He rejoiced that the Federation movement had given them a new name and so helped them to realize their dignity a little more than they used to do. He meant

_ THE NAME OF FREE CHURCHMEN. They used to call themselves chapel-people, and the others church-people. They were, he believed,indebted to the Wesleyans for that word chapel—the only bad thing the Wesleyans ever gave them. (Laughter.) It came originally from the Roman Catholics and it meant an annexe, appendix, or poor relation of the Church. (Laughter.) What it meant now he really did rot know, except that it was a mixture of bad Latin. corrupt French and disparaging ecclesiasticism. When the time came to bury it, he hoped he would be called upon to act as undertaker. (Laughter.) There were the other words, Dissenter' and Nonconformist,both of which were offensive, and equally so because they were negative terms implying that they lived for the very sake of differing and had no positive message to declare. To- day they called themselves very properly Free Church- men—Churchmen, and he claimed to be a Churchman. The Chairman had spoken of the Church of England. He (Mr. Greenough) never did so. They might call him eccentric, singular, or pedantic ; he didn't care. He would not allow one religious body in this land, how- ever great and important it might be, to monopolise the scriptural word, the Church, which belonged to them all. (Hear hear). Nor would he allow one religious body to

MONOPOLISE THE NA.TIOV XL RELIGOUS TERM, the Church of England. They could call it the Episcopal Church, the Established Church, the State Church or the Anglican Church. But the Church of England was truly the Church of Christ in this realm of England—all of it. It belonged to all the believing, Christ-loving people in this world of ours. They claimed all the rights and privileges of Churchmen, except heir bonds which they were not ambitious to wear. (Hear, hear.) They of the Free Churches were all free from the thraldom, the demoralizing rule of the priest. In the Free Churches of this land there was only one Priest—the Priest who diedlfor them, who bought them with His own blood, and who opened the new and living way for them into the holy of holies. They needed no other priest. They all said "We will have no human priest to stand between us and our great High Priest, and get the mastery of our consciences, claiming to have the power of tha confessional, of making the Sacraments valid. We will have no priestly yoke laid upon our lives and consciences. We are free." (Applause). Then they were free, and intended to be free from all interference of Parliament and Govern- ment in matters pertaining to their consciences, to their religions beliefs and their God. They were loyal and law-abiding citizens; none more so. (Hear, hear.) They said to Parliament "We will be obedient to all legisla- tion so long as you keep within your own province, which is civil and political, but as soon as you come out of your own province into that which does not belongito you and undertake to give us a prayer book, to order our creeds, to formulate our worship, to appoint religious teachers over us, then we shall say Take hands off. You have no more to do with those things than the man in the moon or the woman on the back of a half-penny." (Applause.) They were also free from the far heavier yoke of dead Parliaments. Men who had passed away hundreds of years ago had laid down binding and irk- some rules to fetter the freedom of the Spirit of God in coming generations. They were free from that. (Hear, hear ) Nearly all the High Church clergymen, among whom he had some very dear friends, were joining with them in

THE CRY FOR LIBIERTY. They could not bear the yoke of the living Parliament and the dead Parliament. They said it was intoler- able that a Christian Church could not revise its creeds, add to its prayers, or even reform its corruptions without the consent of Parliament. The members of Parliament were of course very good men, but religi- ously they were Catholics,agnosticq schismatics, and he hid ievedtherelwas aiParsee and one Mohammedan. Their High Church friends said very reasonably that .it was intolerable and they demanded that the power of dealing' with the Church should be taken from Parliament and handed over to Convocation—a body of spiritual men. Their demand was perfectly right, only it could never be granted without the penalty—Disestablisbment—and that they were not prepared to pay. He was proud another benefit had been conferred upon them by the great Federation—that it had taught them to respect themselves more. It had given them a new, a larger and truer consciousness of their dignity. It had helped the smallest church in the smallest village of the country to feel that it had behind it half the religious forces of the nation. It had helped those little communities to lift up their beads, and he really thought they wanted help of that kind. He admired the members of the Established Church because of their pride in their church. They gloried in its antiquity, tradition, liturgy, music and buildings, and they were

There was nothing like an Act of that kind for arousing slumbering Nonconformity. He could not speak ill of it—he had .not the heart to speak ill of any thing or person that gas dying. (Laughter). His heart overflowed in pity and compassion towards it. Besides, how could they speak ill of anything about which or whom no one had a good word to say. (Laughter.) He did not know anyone who spoke well of the Act to-day. The Liberal Unionists cursed the day when it saw the light. The Conservatives were inwardly saying it would have been good for that Bill if it had never been born. (Laughter.) Mr. Balfour was actually apologising for the thing on Tuesday night and, as far as he could expressing his regret that he ever touched it. (Laughter.) The Bishops who were so anxious to push it through were at their wits' end what to do with it—now that it would not work. What with Passive Resisters, Welsh County Councillors, and other equally stubborn and unreasonable people it would not go. (Laughter). Bishops had privately been communicating with leading members of the Free Church Federation proposing a con- ference. Why didn't they propose it before ? They had actually been sending the most pitiful Macedonian cries " Come over and help us to work out this thing Come and let us reason in the name of charity." He was loath to refuse an appeal in that sacred name, but before charity there came justice. They could never have any sort of compromise on the matter until simple justice had been done. They must have all the public schools of the kingdom, the rate-supported schools, under public control. (Applause.) They must Insist that in no public rate-supported schools should there be any religious teach- ing distinctive of one denomination given in school hours. (Applause.) Further there should be no religi- ous, doctrinal, denominational tests imposed upon any teacher in the public rate-supported schools. (Applause). It would not take much to make the Bill like that —there were a thousand good things about it. Cardinal Vaughan said when the Act was passed that it was a victory over the Nonconformists. No doubt it was, but it was only a victory in what he called the prelimin- ary skirmish. The

Even as matters turned out later, Warrington should have triumphed if the defence had been up to the mark. But weak tackling at half-back enabled Old- ham to gat another five points in the second half, which ought certainly to have been prevented. 'Twas certainly an ill day for the locals. They bad more than their share of misfortune. Soon after Oldham's initial score, the defence was penetrated and Brookes fell down helplessly a yard from the lire. Could anything have been more aggravating? Then there was quite a shower of points. Fish dropped a goal from a seemingly impossible angle, Isherwood dashed over with a fine try, and Fish added another penalty goal, which gave Warrington th 3 lead. That there was some lusty cheering hardly needs to he said. Warrington's efforts were deserving of success, and hope ran high when the teams turned round.

Unfortunately, the wind was against the homesters in the second half, and this, added to the " blown " condition of the hard-worked forwards gave Oldham many opportunities. Still they did not look like scoring until the Warrington halves let Dixon force his way through. Then there was a knock-on, an appeal by some of the home backs, and Arthur Lees calmly scored behind the posts. Fergusson kicked a second goal, and Warrington's doom was sealed. Bad defensive methods and lax refereeing made the point an easy one for the Oldhamers. They were never much the superior side, and had a lot to thank their lucky star for on Saturday.

More than one member of the home team had an " off-day " in this match. Even Hallam was less effective than usual and ran into the arms of the Oldham forwards much oftener than he should have done. Fish, alone, did himself justice, and he was practically wasted for want of opportunity all after- noon. As I foretold, Hockenhall was a much missed man, and it is not an extravagant thing to suggest that Oldham might nnt have got either try had he been playing. The weak link was at half-bask in both instances, and in this department, A. Lees and Dixon had matters pretty much their own way. Cash and Civil were smart, while Dick Thomas was good and bad by turns. In the scrummage there was not much to choose until the second half when the Oldham men were distinctly fresher. As a team too, they kicked much better than Warrington, but then the homesters, bar Fish and Preston, never could "punch." The victory has placed Oldham well in the running for the Championship once more, and they now share with Broughton the top position. The Rangers had the advantage of a match in hand, however, and are at present the favourites. Bradford once more fell under the spell of the blue and white jersey and sustained a severe defeat at Halifax which puts them out of court for a while. They would give this land no rest, they would give no Parliament or Government any rest —whatever colour it be; they would give themselves no rest, and they would use all means they deemed legitimate to fight this new religious test Act, as inquitous as any that was ever passed, this new endow- ment of two religious communions—the Episcopal and the Roman Catholic—and this new outrage upon the Nonconformist conscience, until the Act had been so amended, re-shaped, re-created by the changes he had indicated—so changed that its own mother would not know it—or else sent back into the chambers of injustice and priestcraft from which it came. MOTOR 'NOTES. An unusual feature of the OlympiaSho w was a silver- plated 30 h.p. chassis displayed upon the stand of the Simms-Welbeck Company.

Batley are wakening up as the Cup-ties come round. They have won two matches in succession this last week—Hull Kingston Rovers on Saturday, and Widnes on Tuesday, succumbing at Mount Pleasant.

The 'Judges Box" in the railed-in grass enclosure of the Motor Car Co.'s stand was another unique feature of the show and created considerable attention and interest. The Prince of Wales expressed himself much pleased with it on the occasion of his visit to the show.

To-day's matches are of vital importance to the South-West Lancashire Clubs, and particularly to Warrington, the holders of the Cup. Warrington have to journey to Wigan and must win there in order to get ihe better of the home club, and Leigh. The latter are due at Runcorn and will find their task much more difficult than they probably imagine. Warrington, Wigan, and Leigh are all well in the running for the cup, and to-day's results will have an important, if not a final, J)earing on its ultimate destruction.

The Siddeley Car, too, which completed its 5,000 miles trial on the road just before Olympia opened, was invariably surrounded by a little crowd of spectators. Tne engine had been dismantled for inspection, and gear-wheels -lifferential, Stz., were all so exposed that the public could easily see for themselves how slight had been the wear upon these essential parts despite the amount of mileage covered. The Siddeley is undoubtedly a fine car. It is fast on the level, very good on hills, and is exceptionally silent in running.

There was quits a'surprising number of exhibits in the Motor Boat Section. " iNfotor boating promises to become a most popular pastime.

Warrington will, of course, make a great effort to- day. It will be a surprise to most of their supporters to find the same fifteen chosen. Evidently the com- mittee have not the courage to make experiments or there would have been some change at half-back. Either Brookes or Fish could have taken Davies'place, 1 and Harris come in again. Some way of giving Fish more play ought to he devised, and I hope Oaptain Hallam will rearrange his forces at Wigan rather than have the crack threequarter marking time along the touch-line.

All the accessories were relegated to the large gallery which runs round the building, and amongst them many clever novelties were to be seen. The "Security" non- skidding device shewn at Gamage's interesting stand will doubtless appeal to those motorists who live in constant dread of side-slip but are averse to usink the customary non-skids formed of metal-studded leather bands and vulcanised on to the outer cover. Instead of being made, as are these, in one piece to cover the tyre completely, the "Security" is quite small, and, like the tyre-repairing gaiter, can be strapped on to the rim obliquely between two spokes. Thus, five or six are used on the one wheel. This contrivance certainly dis- poses of the grehtest drawback of the ordinary leather band, for the vulcanising process is dispensed with and thus there is no damage inflicted by this means upon the rubber of the tyre.

The full team should be : Hallam,full back; Fish, Isherwood,Brookes and Kenyon,threequarters; Davies and Bate, half backs; Boardman, Thomas, Shugars, Belton, Naylor, Jolley, Swift, and Cook, forwards.

The " A " team managed to win at Oldham ty a try to nil, Berry being the scorer. To-day a battle royal should be seen at Wilderspool, and almost as much interest is being shown in the encounter between the juniors as between the seniors. Wigan "A" sustained their only defeat at the hands of Warrington, and the latter are still their only rivals for the Combination leadership. With the men Warrington are able to put in the field to-day they should be able to give the visitors their quietus. The twelve are : Knight ; Harris, Dickenson, Berry, and Jordan ; J. Davies and W. Bate ; Coleman, Harmer, Foster, Lunt, and Morrison, forwards.

The H. F. vulcaniser for the use of the amateur was on view. It is certainly extremely handy besides being so simpleto manipulate than any raw amateur should be able to effect most satisfactory repairs with its assist- ance. The size designed to be carried in the car so that wayside repairs can be quickly and satisfactorily accomplished is particularly suitable for such a case,and no motorist who has 'suffered from tire troubles by the roadside should fail to give one of these capital little vulcanisers a practical trial. The price is quite a moderate one.

One of the most useful novelties is the " tell-tale ' for the rear lamps fitted to motor cars. Every driver has experienced the worry of being uncertain whether that regulation light is still going, and his troubles are greatly increased in this direction when there are no passengers to help him. Dunhill's " Tell-Tale' " how- ever, solves the difficulty, for when this is used a red light is automatically. switched on the dashboard directly the rear lamp Is extinguished.

for the poor display is that the ground was very lumpy and soft.

-- The first fifteen minutes saw the visitors having the better of the play, owing to the home backs being off colour, and when Rogers scored from a scramble in the Stockton Heath goal mouth, things did not look too bright for the home side. After this reverse the Stockton forwards managed to get going, and after several hot attacks on the Huytou goal, R. Pierpoint made the scores level with a good shot. From now till half-time Huyton had rather the better of the exchanges, and nearly added two more goals from scrambles in the home goal. Half-time I score, 1-1.

As Huyton had been playing down the slope,the home team were 1:10'V fairly confident of reversing the result of the match at Ilnyton, when they suffered defeat by 2-6. It seemed almost a pity to see a player of the calibre of Rogers wasted in the forward line, when one knows what a skilful player he is at half or full-back. The second half went in favour of Stockton Heath, although it was a long time before S. Baxter managed to put on what proved to be the winning goal. Close on time Rogers took up the centre half position, Lewis taking his place among the forwards, and these changes nearly took effect, as Lewis came very near scoring on two occasions.

This week one must praise the forwards, who played a good game, while being badly fed by their backs. That the forwards managed to play as well as they did is a wonder, seeing that their backs thought it their special duty to dribble round three or four men, if possible. Without a doubt the backs and halves who have been the great instrumsnts of our success this season, were the weaker part of the team last week, and hope it will be a long time before they take "another day off."

It was only the other week that the departure of It. Fairclough for South Africa was announced, and now W. G. Longdin is going out to Rangoon. As the notice has been too short to get up anything worthy of such a good fellow, I take the liberty of expressing to him in this column' our heartiest thanks for the way he has worked for the club, not only this year, but in the past, "May the best day of his past be the worst day of his future."

The Second team journeyed to Ifuyton with rather a scratch team, and were defeated by 5-l. Things are going badly with the second team, and it is to be hoped that the players will be keener than has lately been the case. The goal for Stockton Heath was scored by W. Howard.

Without a doubt the most interesting match played last Saturday was the Bowdon and Northern match at Bowdon. The game which was very even ended in a draw of two goals each. Bebington visited West Derby and only managed to win by 1-0. Chester visited New Brighton and were easily beaten, the home side winning by no less than thirteen goals to one. Alderley Edge were visited by Timperley, and came out on top by 3-0. The home side all round were much smarter than the visitors. Sale were visited by Kersal, and seeing that Kersal were poorly represented in the forwards, the defeat of the visitors by 1-0 was not very brilliant Latchford were at home to Sefton and an even game ended in a draw of two goals each.

TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL. Corporation and Fair Contracts.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council was held in Bold-street Wesleyan Schools on Tuesday evening, Mr. J. l'odnaore, jun., presiding.

MR. PIERPOINT CRITICISED. - Mr. GREALEY pointed out that recently resolutions in support of the Sunday Closing Bill were forwarded to the Archbishop of Canterbury, the Home Secretary, and Mr. Robert Pierpoint. M.P. Acknowledgments were received on behalf of the Archbishop and Home Secretary but Mr. Pierpoint did not reply. Whether that Council was beneath the borough member's notice or not he (Mr. Grealey) did not know but the mem- bers of the Bakers' Society, who were interested in the matter, felt it keenly that they should be treated in such a fashion. The CHAIRMAN said his trade had taken up the ques- tion and nearly 100,000 signatures had been obtained to a petition in support of the Bill. He was sorry Mr. Pierpoint had GOt replied. He ought to have done so if only in consideration of support he had received in the past from members of the Hairdressers' Society. THE RUSSIAN STRUGGLE FOR FREEDOM. Under this heading the National Labour Representation Committee forwarded a circular appealing for subscrip- tions to a fund in aid of those who had suffered through the massacre in St. Petersburg and in support of the Russian working-class movement. The tyrannical barbarities of the Russian Government had been pro- claimed in the columns of the press and unless aid was forthcoming immediately there was a danger that brutal military force might be successful in crushing out the movement for constitutional liberty and the right of combination. It was stated that the local branch of the Labour Representation Committee had discussed the matter, land the Council decided to join with that organization in issuing an appeal through the press.

===== 0002601_19050218_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
RESIGNATION OF THE TREASURER..

Mr. A. WARBURTON, of the Sheet Metal Workers' Society and treasurer of the Council, wrote tendering his resignation from the Council and stating that Mr. W. H. Richardson had been elected a delegate in his place. It was decided to appoint a new treasurer at the next meeting.

LABOUR REPRESENTATION COMMITTEE.

Nfr. BUCKLEY reported that the chief subject under consideration at the meeting of this committee was that of reorganization. On the recommendation of the Executive Committee it was decided to hold a Labour demonstration and the committee was empowered to proceed with the arrangements. The report was accepted. THE UNEMPLOYED QUESTION. _ _ _

The SECRETARY (Councillor -Stott) presented an interesting report of the proceedings at a special confer- ence held at Liverpool to consider this question. He stated that 380 delegates.attended and the opinion, as expressed in resolutions, was that every man who could work ought to have work provided for him by the State or municipality, not as a charity but as a human right. A resolution was adopted in favour of the provision of meals for under-fed school children at the public expense, the Labour members of Parliament being asked to introduce a Bill during the present session giving effect to that demand. Labour Town Councillors were also called upon to urge their Councils to provide money for the work at once, pending a final settlement of the question by Parlia- ment. Councillor Stott said he had never attended a gathering at which more enthusiasm and ability were shown.

The report was adopted and a vote of thanks was accorded to Councillor Stott.

CORPORATION AND FAIR CONTRACTS. The SECRETARY reported that the special sub-com- mittee appointed by the Town Council to revise the standing orders affecting fair contracts had met on two occasions. The last meeting lasted a considerable time but even then the business had to be ad- journed again. They hoped at the next meeting to settle the standing orders which they were prepared to submit to the General Purposes Committee. Of course, he did not mean that the question would be satisfactorily settled from the Trades Council's point of view. They could not, perhaps, expect to get all they wanted, and they would have to be satisfied with what was forthcoming. Mr. BUCKLEY : Have the committee accepted out- side evidence?

Councillor STOTT : I mentioned the matter at the last meeting, but they thought it better to draft out some resolutions before they decided to accept any out- side evidence.

LA DOUR REPRESENTATION CONFERENCE.

An exhaustive report on th.e-is:.atic"-)",;ffr-tbOiir Repre- sentation Committee's confere-ce at Liverpool was presented by Mr. 0. Buckley. He dealt at some length with the question of the committee's constitution, and said the delegates showed clearly that they did not want any alteration that would effect the policy of independence. The report was adopted and Mr. Buckley was thanked.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Lecture on Dr. Parker. At the Primitive Methodist Church, Knutsford-road,. on Tuesday, night, the Rev. Dr. Scurrah, Congregational Minister, of Leveushulme. delivered his popular lecture on" The life and work of the late Dr. Parker." It is a matter for regret there were not more present to list a to the eloquent treatment of the subject. Those who were there were held spell-bound2for a full hour as of poured forth in eloquent sentences the life story of this great and good man—a story which, as the lecturer said must remain for the historian of the next genera- tion to record. The lecture consisted principally of three parts, viz., the early life ; call to the Ministry and his Manchester and especially his London ministra- tion; each being set forth in a most vivid, lively and intensely interesting manner. Great thanks are indeed due to the Rev, gentlemen who, has most generously given his services to the friends worshipping at the Knutsford-road Chapel. A cordial vote of thanks to the lecturer and chairman (Mr. J. Griffiths) was proposed by the circuit minister Rev. C. L.'Stowe, seconded by Mr. T. Metcalfe, and being carried brought the meeting to a close, A collection was taken for the organ fund.. Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. ' A remarkable and interesting meeting was held on Sunday, when an ex-burglar was the speaker, from Man- chester. He came as a substitute for the appointed speaker. _there was a very good gathering of members, Mr. R. Atkinson presiding.—The Chairman said at one time he should not have liked to stand by the speaker's side, but now that the grace of God had done its work, , he could stand with him with pleasure and safety in a meeting of that kind. Mr. Atkinson referred to the . happy work of the P.S.A. Society for the last twelve years. They had distributed as prizes over 10,300 books at a cost of £l,OOO in that time. Then what a power , for good must the many prayers, addresses, and, lessons have been. He bad now great pleasure in calling upon Mr. Frank Ford, an ex-criminal, to address them. Mr. Frank Ford who was received with cheers said he had led many away from Christ and he was now trying to bring them hack. In many cases he had succeeded by the help of God, and others were coming home to Christ. He was now engaged in the Rev Collier's Mission in Manchesteraind related ninny experi- ences of midnight work amongst the fallen,and the happy changes which had been wrought in the lives of many found in sin. When he concluded there was prolonged applause. Many of the brethren after service expressed the wish that Mr. Ford might be asked to come again. Mr. NV. Beswick and Mr. A. Healey also took part in the meeting. Miss Annie Travis sang "When the children are asleep." Mr. W. H. Payton INlus. Bac. presided at the or_an. Prize lists were given out. vssis ad Billiards Rev. H. Dunnico and the Torrey-Alexander' Mission. " Artificial Excitement." • The Rev. H. Dunnico preached c• ded congre- gation t the Parr Hall oa .euing on "The Welsh and other revivals." Basing his remarks on the words : "These men are not drunken as ye suppose," the preacher said that the subject of revivals was not only the most interesting of present-day topics, but also one upon which diverse opinions were held. He intended to speak somewhat plainly, and no doubt he should say things not agreeable to them all. He did not think that they (the congregation) would accuse him of being a gloomy preacher, still, he dare , not shut his eyes to the signs of the Ames. Events were taking place in the history of nations which were a danger and a menace to civilization. In his opinion the vast international fabric was likely to be shaken by a series of cataclysms such as had never occurred in all history, and this revival was one way in which Provi- dence sought to equip and discipline them for the con- flict. Proceeding to deal with the revival led by Dr. Torrey and Mr. Alexander, Mr. Dunnico said that he had very little to say in its favour, and would refrain from saying much in its disfavour. No doubt some would differ strongly with him, and they were at liberty to do so. He had been to the mission himself, and simply desired to give his own opinions and state his own impressions. Mr. Alexander deserved all praise, but Dr. Torrey was only a very ordinary per4on. He spoke in a loud, harsh tone, with hardly a note of feeling. His address was plain and commonplace, and had much in it that many people would resent. There was little if any appeal, much denunciation. The so-called revival was simply artificial excitement, deliberately worked upon on pre-arranged methods. Reverence and devotion were conspicuous by their absence, and the atmosphere was filled with a commotion more typical of the market-place than the House of God. He did not say the meetings were not productive of good. That would be untrue; still, they would not appeal to him. He might be wrong, but it seemed to him that if, instead of denouncing smoking, dancing and theatre-going, he would denounce the apathy of Christian people in the face of the appall- ing social* and political evils of this land, he would render more effective service. Referring to the cost of the great mission, which was estimated at nearly £20,000, Mr. Dunnico said that many people thought that this money might have been spent in more profit- able ways. Perhaps they were right, but so far as he was concerned, the money was of secondary importance. The very people who criticized the spending of this money, would spend more than £20,000 next Saturday afternoon on football, and surely the most adverse critic must admit that the mission would do more towards the moral uplifting of the people than football matches. No; what troubled him most was the elaborate organiza- tions. It seemed as though the Spirit of God had to be coaxed by large choirs and huge halls. In London they in the ifsotermrs, had thousands of churches, thousands of professing and yet two men had to be imported from America great halls had to be engaged, large choirs organized at of receiving salaries, tremendous cost, before tr.e people of this land could be revived. If the churches were born of the Spirit—and a hundreds Christi an Christians, f thousandsthousands o I Christian pounds church had no right to exist if it were not—all things. , were possible. Every movement had its frayed edges, and the revival in Wales was no exception to that rule,', The " Lancet " had ventured to predict that , this revival would produce a form of in- sanity, but although it was dangerous to differ ' with doctors, he would venture to predict that the insanity produced by this revival would be infinitesimar compared to the insanity it weuld save through stop- ping the drink. Still, if the worst was true about the Welsh revival,no thoughtful man would treat the move- ment with indifference on that account. The revival in Wales differed entirely from the revival of Dr. Torrey. It possessed signs of genuineness which the other lacked. It was local in its origin, a native in its growth: there was no preaching; no hymn books, no choirs, no organs, no advertising. And yet everybody was preaching and singing, and the movement was spreading. People asked how it began, and the reply was, "It began in Evan Roberts." That was not true. Evan Roberts was the Product, not the origin. It originated in the heart of God, and had come in answer to prayer. Some were asking would this revival visit Warrington ? Most assuredly it would if they desired it. Prayer was as potent in Warrington as in Wales. But as in Wales, so in Warrington, the revival must come through the Church to be effective, and more than a passing breeze. The churches, therefore, must pre- pare themselves, and all that was in God's way must be cleared away. God might not visit them in exactly the same way as in Wales. He always manifested Himself through the natural temperament, and we could not have the poetic fire and fervour of Welsh revival. But He would come in His own way, when we were ready, and the blessing would be as great.

THE " EIGHTY " CLUB.

In view of the possibility of a General Election, this year will doubtless be marked by great political activity and we have been asked to remind those of our readers who are on the Committees of Political or Literary Societies of the work of the Eighty Club. This club consists of e number of gentlemen willing to assist the Liberal and Free Trade cause by speaking at Local Clubs, Debating Societies, Social Unions or Public Meetings as well as by: delivering lectures on subjects of current political interests. No fee whatever is charged. The club is preparing to send speakers not only to larger meetings but also to villages and to Ward meetings. Sir Edward Grey has been nominated President of the club for 1905 and the Secretary is Mr. R. C. Hawkin of 3, Hare Court, Temple, London, B.C. to whom all letters should be addressed. COMMTIRCIAL FAlLURES.—According,to Kemp's Mer- cantile Gazette, the total number of Commercial Failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, Feb. 10th, was 198, viz. :—Nest Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette, 101; Deeds of Arrangement registered, 97. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were : Bankruptcies, 101 ; Deeds of Arrangements; 98—Total 202 ; being a decrease of 4. The total number of Commercial Failures recorded during the 6 weeks of the present year is 1,053 : the total number re- corded in the corresponding 6 weeks of last year waS 1,026 showing an increase of 27.

AWONDERFUL DISCOVERY.—This is the aRe of research and experiment, when all nature, so to speak, is ransacked by the scientific for the comfort and happiness of man. Science has indeed made giant strides during the past century, and among the—by no rneamilleast impertant—discoveries in medicine comes that of THRRAPION. This preparation is unquestionably one of the most) gen- uine and relialsle Patent Medicines ever introduced, and has we understand, been used in the Continental Hospitals by Ricord, Rostan, 7obert, Velpeau, Maisonneuve, the well- ; known Chassslgnaa, and indeed by all who are regarded as authorities in such matters, including the celebrated Lalle- mend, and Roux, by whom it was some time !linen uniformly adopted, and that it is worthy the attention of those who require such a remedy we think there is no doubt. From the time of Aristotle downwards a potent agent in the removal of these diseases has, like the famed philosopher's stone been the object of search of some hopeful generous minds ; and far beyond the mere power—if such could ever ' have been discovered—of transmuting the baser metal into gold is surely the discovery of a remedy so potent as to replenish the failing energies in the one case, and In the other so effectually, speedily and safely to expel from the system the poisons of acquired or Inherited disease In all their protean forms as to leave no taint or trace behind, Such is the new French Remedy THERAPION, which may certainly rmk with, if not take precedence of many of the discoveries of our day, about which no little Estentation and noise have been maade, and the extensive and ever-increas- ing demand that has been created for this medicine wherever introduced appears to prove that It is destined to cast int ) oblivion all those questionable remedies that were formerly the sole reliance of medical men. _ best season we have known for years, and may pros- perity, sociabality and goodfellowship continue to be the motto of the Wacrington Cycling Club. Mr. W. Lirro,..l moved the adoption of the report, remarking that it chronicled the club's doings in a way which reflected the greatest credit upon both the club and Mr. Pickavance. Their Secretary's literary abili- ties were indeed so great that one wondered that he had not achieved a very much higher post, but his time was so occupied with the work of the club that he had not the chance. (Laughter.) There was not the slightest doubt that they had had a most successful season but had it been twenty times as successful there would have been nothing lacking on Mr. Pickavance's part to have described it. (Hear, hear.) Mr. W. TAYLOR seconded, and the report was unani- mously adopted. THE FINANCES. The Hots. TREASURER (Mr. F. Garnett) reported that the receipts, including a balance brought forward of £3 3s. 3d., amounted to £2B 17s. 3d, The expenditure was £23 Os. 4d., leaving a balance in hand of £5 7s. 11d, In addition they had a balance in the bank of £8 185. 9d., making a total to the credit of the club of .814 6s. Bd. Mr. D DAVIES moved the adoption of the balance sheet. In seconding Mr. PICKAVANCE said the season had, from a financial point of view, been one of the most favourable they had ever experienced. Out of ninety members, only two had had to be struck off for non- payment of subscriptions. That,he thought, showed that they had the backbone of a good muster who would stick to the club. The position of affairs were highly creditable to ,the treasurer. (Hear, hear). The statement was adopted. ELECTION OF OFFICERS. Mr. W. H. BELLIAN proposed the re-election of Mr. John Naylor as president. They were proud to have such a gentleman at the head of the club, and ther were sure he was equally proud to preside over an organiza- tion of the standing of Warrington C.C. Mr. BOOTIE seconded and the proposition was heartily carried. The other elections were as 'follows :—Honorary vice-presidents, the Mayor (Alderman Bolton), Alder- man Pierpoint, Messrs. J. C. Parr, Roger C. Parr, T. F. Kiernar, IL Pierpoint, M.P., A. H. Crosfield, E. Houghton and T. Mather; active vice-presidents,Messrs. W. H. Bellian, W. Brown, A. Booth, J. Critchley, D. I Davies, J. •L. Davenport, Dr. Kisby, W. Litton, J. IPodmore,W. Mounfleld,and C. H. Wood; hon. secretary, Mr. E. Pickavance; hon. treasurer, Mr. F. Garnett; captain,, Mr. D. Hodge; sub-captain;Mr. J. G. Chappell; auditors, Messrs. W. Litton and J. E. Grounds; N.C.U. delegates, Messrs. E. Pickava,nce and A. Booth; com- mittee, Messrs. J. L. Davenport, G. H. Smith, W. H. Bellian, W. Taylor, J. T. Sampson, W. Brown, A. Booth, and J. Podmore. A vote of thanks to the Chairman for presiding con- cluded the meeting. A LIVERPOOL POLISHER'S PERIL. Zam-Buk Cures Poisoned and Ulcerated Arms Very few of those housewives who admire the beautiful work of the French polisher think of the physical suffering which has sometimes to be endured so as to give such a finish to the furniture. The chemicals used have frequentlya serious effect upon the man's hands and arms, and in a recent Liverpool case, blood poisoning and ulceration was the result. The victim was Mr. Joseph H. Jones, of 2, Raglan-terrace, Alma- street, Everton, Liverpool, and he has given an account of his sufferings, and of his cure by using Zam-Buk, to a Liverpool reporter: - "About seven or eight years ago," said Mr. Jones, "my fingers got covered with blisters in consequence of a strong polish I had' using. These blisters became very irritable, and in a time developed into ulcers, which caused me to seek medical aid. For several years I was taking medicine, but I never seemed to properly recover. Shortly after Caristmas the affection spread to my arms and lees, and I was quite unable to work. I had to lay up. My fingers were swollen' stiff, and were all out of shape whilst my arms had to be I bandaged twice a day, and was helpless as a child. My wife had to wash, dress, and Iced me, and I settled down as a confirmed "About this time my wife had been using Zam-Buk successfully for her chapped bands,and she prevailed on me to give it a trial. I did so, with a result that can only be described as magical. With absolute truth, I may say that from the first my hands and arms com- menced to improve, and at the end of a fortnight I was completely cured. The swellingshad gone,the stiffness vanished and the sort places were all healed up. I attribute this entirely to the soothing, antiseptic and curative powers of Zam-Buk, and when I think of the nights of sleeplessness I have suffered, of the agony I endured, my cure seems incredible." i Zam-Buk s invaluable for poisoned wounds, fester- ing sores, ulcers, eczema, piles, psoriasis,boils, pimples, sores, rash, bad legs, cuts, bruises, burns,scalds,chatings, itch, cramp, stiff joints, raw chins, black-heads, scalp irritation, sore heads, aching feet, weak ankles, sprains, stiffness, swollen knees, inflamed patches, and sunburn. Price Is. lid. per box,or 25.9 d. large family size (25.9 d. box contains nearly four times the Is. 1d.); of all chemists, or post free for same prices direct from the Zam-Buk Co., 4, Red Cross-street, London, R.C.

Lymm Volunteers at Dinner. The prizes gained by members of the Lymm Volun- teer Company were distributed by the Rector (Rev. G. B. Thurston) at the annual dinner held on Saturday evening. A. large gathering was presided over by Captain J.F.G. Groves who said the company was now stronger than it had ever been since its forma- tion in 1863—The Rev. G. B. Thurstdn, in the corrse lof a brief speech, said it was the duty of every man to make himself efficient in military drill—A successful smoking concert was afterwards held.
